\title{Thermodynamic Potentials, Ensembles, and Response Functions\\
\large A working guide for graduate students}
\author{}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
These notes trace one logical chain from a statistical ensemble to a measurable
response function. (i) The fundamental relation $U(S,V,N)$ encodes everything;
(ii) Legendre transforms trade a variable for its conjugate and produce the
thermodynamic potentials, each minimised at equilibrium under its own
constraints; (iii) each ensemble computes one such potential as the logarithm of
a partition function; (iv) second derivatives of that potential are response
functions and, by the fluctuation--response structure of the ensemble, equal
variances. Several points routinely passed over are treated explicitly: the
logical status of $\dbar Q=T\,dS$, the distinction between the Legendre transform
and the variational principle it is often conflated with, and the fact that the
constant-$\mu$ and constant-$N$ heat capacities are different functions. A final
strand runs the argument backwards: a phase transition exists only in the
thermodynamic limit, so no finite simulation displays one, and
Section~\ref{sec:fss} turns that obstruction into a measuring instrument.
Section~\ref{sec:filters} descends to the microscopic side: for independent
levels in the grand canonical ensemble every observable is the single-particle
spectrum seen through its own filter, all centred on $\mu$ and all of width set
by $T$ alone, but of different shapes --- so that the entropy counts the levels
that are undecided while the heat capacity is exactly blind to them.
Peripheral derivations and numerical practice are collected in the appendices.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Notation and conventions}
\label{sec:notation}

$k_B$ is Boltzmann's constant and $\beta=1/k_BT$. We write $\dbar Q$ and
$\dbar W$ (rather than $dQ$, $dW$) for one-forms that are not the differential of
any state function (Appendix~\ref{app:exact}). Work is counted positive when done
\emph{on} the system, so $\dbar W=-P\,dV$ for reversible compression.

Four notational collisions are worth flagging, since each is standard and none can
be avoided without inventing symbols. $\Omega$ is the grand potential throughout,
the microcanonical multiplicity being written $W(E,V,N)$. $\Delta$ is the gap
exponent in Section~\ref{sec:critical} and the isothermal--isobaric partition
function in Section~\ref{sec:ensembles}. $\omega$ is the grand potential density
$\Omega/V$, a measurement frequency in $c(\omega)$, and the leading
correction-to-scaling exponent of Section~\ref{sec:fss}. And $\beta$ is the
inverse temperature except in lists of critical exponents, where it is the
order-parameter exponent $m\sim|t|^{\beta}$; where that could be ambiguous the
inverse temperature is written out or carried as $K=J/k_BT$.

\section{The fundamental relation}
\label{sec:totaldiff}

For a simple compressible system with variable particle number, all equilibrium
information sits in one function of entirely extensive variables,
\[
U=U(S,V,N).
\]
We call $U$ the internal energy; \emph{fundamental relation} refers to $U$ in
these natural variables specifically, and \emph{potential} is used collectively
for the whole family generated below. Its differential defines the conjugate
intensive variables,
\[
T\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial S}\right)_{V,N},\qquad
-P\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_{S,N},\qquad
\mu\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial N}\right)_{S,V},
\]
giving
\[
\boxed{\;dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
The three terms are the thermal, mechanical and chemical channels of energy
exchange. In a condensed-matter setting $-P\,dV$ generalises to
$\sum_{ij}V\sigma_{ij}\,d\epsilon_{ij}$ for elastic strain, to
$\mathcal{H}\,dM$ for magnetic work, to $\mathbf{E}\cdot d\mathbf{P}$ for
dielectric work; each added work mode adds one natural variable, so that
$U=U(S,V,N,M,\dots)$. Everything below goes through with $(-P,V)$ replaced by the
relevant conjugate pair.

Four assumptions are hidden in those three lines. They are of different
character, and each fails somewhere identifiable.

\paragraph{(A1) Single-valuedness of $U$.} That $U$ is a function of the state at
all is empirical content, not mathematics. Its basis is Joule's observation that
the work needed to carry an adiabatically enclosed system between two states is
path-independent, which permits $U$ to be defined, up to an additive constant, on
the space of equilibrium states.

Where it fails, it fails badly: a ferromagnet driven around a hysteresis loop
returns to the same $(T,\mathcal{H})$ with a different magnetisation and a
different internal energy. This is not a failure of statistical mechanics --- the
Hamiltonian, the microstates and the Boltzmann weight are untouched, and the loop
is a sequence of metastable states whose relaxation is kinetically obstructed.
What fails is the premise of this section, that the equilibrium state is a
single-valued function of the control variables. On the equilibrium branch,
reached by waiting out the relaxation or by field-cooling, $U$ is single valued
and everything below applies; around the loop there is no function of state to
differentiate.

\paragraph{(A2) Completeness of the variable list.} The state postulate asserts
that an equilibrium state of a simple, one-component, single-phase system is
fixed by $(S,V,N)$ and nothing else, so the state space is a three-dimensional
manifold. The count is one thermal variable, plus one per independent work mode,
plus one per chemical species.

The content of the postulate is timescale separation. Let $\xi$ be a macroscopic
collective variable --- an order parameter, the extent of a chemical reaction, a
configurational coordinate in a glass --- with relaxation time $\tau_\xi$ much
longer than the microscopic collision times
($10^{-13}$--$10^{-11}\,\mathrm{s}$), and let $\tau_{\rm obs}$ be the duration of
the measurement. Three regimes must be distinguished, and only the middle one
adds a variable.

\begin{itemize}
\item $\tau_\xi\ll\tau_{\rm obs}$: $\xi$ is equilibrated. Its value is already a
function of $(S,V,N)$, fixed by $\mathcal{A}=0$ below. The state postulate stands
as written. All internal variables of an ordinary simple fluid are of this type,
which is why $(S,V,N)$ suffices.

\item $\tau_\xi\gg\tau_{\rm obs}$: $\xi$ is frozen and must be promoted. It holds
one definite macroscopic value throughout the measurement, and every fast degree
of freedom has equilibrated \emph{at that fixed} $\xi$, so $T$, $P$ and $\mu$ are
as sharp as ever. The system is in \emph{constrained equilibrium} and the state
space is four-dimensional:
\[
U=U(S,V,N;\xi),\qquad
dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN-\mathcal{A}\,d\xi ,
\qquad
\mathcal{A}\equiv-\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial\xi}\right)_{S,V,N} ,
\]
with $\mathcal{A}$ the generalized force conjugate to
$\xi$.\footnote{In the chemical literature $\mathcal{A}$ is called the
\emph{affinity}; it is an ordinary generalized force and no separate concept is
involved.} Physical instances: a glass quenched below $T_g$; an alloy with a
frozen long-range order parameter; the ortho/para ratio in solid hydrogen, which
relaxes over days.

Nothing here contradicts the second law. Thermodynamics is being applied to the
\emph{constrained} system, in which $\xi$ is an externally imposed label rather
than a free internal variable, exactly as $V$ is a label imposed by a wall; all
the machinery below is valid at fixed $\xi$. Releasing the constraint, which
happens on the timescale $\tau_\xi$, then lets $\xi$ move in the direction the
second law prescribes, producing entropy at the rate
$T\,d\sigma=\mathcal{A}\,d\xi\geq0$, until $\mathcal{A}=0$ and the unconstrained
equilibrium of the first regime is reached.

\item $\tau_\xi\sim\tau_{\rm obs}$: no state description at all. Here $\xi$
neither tracks the constraints nor holds still, no equilibrium manifold exists on
which to define $U$, and state functions must be given up in favour of
frequency-dependent response, $c(\omega)=c'(\omega)-ic''(\omega)$
(Section~\ref{sec:clausius}).
\end{itemize}


The natural variables are therefore of two kinds. Those protected by a
conservation law or by a wall --- energy, volume, particle number --- need not
relax at all: their values are imposed from outside and the system cannot change
them. Everything else must relax fast compared with $\tau_{\rm obs}$, or else be
frozen and promoted. The diffusive time $\tau\sim L^{2}/D$ for a conserved
density to become spatially \emph{uniform} is a condition on how long one must
wait before calling a sample homogeneous; it is not a property of the natural
variables, which are the spatially integrated totals.

All of these are macroscopic averages over $\sim10^{23}$ microscopic degrees of
freedom, with relative fluctuations of order $N^{-1/2}$: it is their
\emph{number}, not their sharpness, that the state postulate is about.

\paragraph{(A3) Independence of the variables.} The symbol $(\partial U/\partial
S)_{V,N}$ presupposes that $S$ can be varied with $V$ and $N$ held fixed.
Formally, $(S,V,N)$ must be a coordinate chart: the three functions must be
\emph{functionally independent}\footnote{\label{fn:funcind}\textbf{Functional
independence.} Functions $f_1,\dots,f_n$ are functionally \emph{dependent} near a
point if some non-constant relation $\Phi(f_1,\dots,f_n)=0$ holds identically
there, and functionally independent otherwise. This is not linear independence of
the functions: $x$ and $x^{2}$ are linearly independent and functionally
dependent. The practical test is differential: $f_1,\dots,f_n$ are functionally
independent at $p$ if and only if $df_1,\dots,df_n$ are linearly independent
there, equivalently the Jacobian has rank $n$. Differentiating
$\Phi(f_1,\dots,f_n)=0$ gives $\sum_i(\partial\Phi/\partial f_i)\,df_i=0$, which
is precisely that linear dependence.} so that $\{dS,dV,dN\}$ is a basis of the
cotangent space and the expansion coefficients in $dU$ are unique.

This is not automatic, and thermodynamics supplies its own counterexample. The
intensive triple $(T,P,\mu)$ is functionally dependent: the Gibbs--Duhem relation
$S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu=0$ derived below is precisely the differential form of a
relation $\Phi(T,P,\mu)=0$, with coefficients $(S,-V,N)$ that never vanish. One
may therefore not expand any quantity in $dT$, $dP$ and $d\mu$ simultaneously,
and a symbol such as $(\partial\,\cdot/\partial T)_{P,\mu}$ has no meaning ---
which is also why the ``fourth'' Legendre transform $U-TS+PV-\mu N$ vanishes
identically instead of yielding a new potential. The extensive variables escape
this because they can be prescribed by independent physical operations (wall off
a volume, admit particles, add heat), whereas the intensive ones are already
derivatives of the fundamental relation and inherit relations among themselves.

\paragraph{(A4) Continuous differentiability.} The equation
$dU=\sum_i(\partial U/\partial x_i)\,dx_i$ asserts that the increment is captured
by a \emph{linear} map,
\[
U(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{h})-U(\mathbf{x})=\sum_i a_ih_i+o(|\mathbf{h}|),
\]
which is total (Fr\'echet) differentiability. Existence of the partial
derivatives alone does not deliver it: they may exist everywhere and yet the
increment fail to be linear in the direction of approach, in which case there is
no gradient, no differential and no tangent plane. Appendix~\ref{app:diff}
works through the standard counterexample and states the Landau symbols used
throughout.

The sufficient condition is familiar: if the partial derivatives exist in a
neighbourhood and are continuous there, then $U$ is of class
$C^{1}$\footnote{\label{fn:Cn}\textbf{Differentiability classes, and the word
``smooth''.} $f$ is of class $C^{n}$ on an open set if all its partial
derivatives up to order $n$ exist and are continuous there; $C^{0}$ is continuity
alone and $C^{\infty}$ is $C^{n}$ for every $n$. The classes are strictly nested:
$|x|$ is $C^{0}$ but not $C^{1}$, and $x|x|$ is $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$. On a
manifold the definition is applied in any chart, and the class is chart
independent. Two facts are used repeatedly: $f\in C^{1}$ implies $f$ is totally
differentiable, so $df$ exists as a one-form; $f\in C^{2}$ implies the Hessian is
symmetric, which is what makes the Maxwell relations available.
\emph{Smooth} is used here in the physicists' sense --- as differentiable as the
argument requires --- which is almost always far less than $C^{\infty}$; where
the required class is sharp it is stated. The distinction matters immediately:
this section turns on $U$ being $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$ at a first-order
transition, and on $G$ being merely $C^{0}$ there.} and the expansion is valid. Translated into physics,
the requirement is that
\[
T,\quad -P,\quad \mu \qquad\text{be \emph{continuous} functions of state,}
\]
since they are the partial derivatives. (A4) is thus a physical condition on the
equation of state, not an analyst's fussiness.

\subsection{Behaviour at a first-order transition}

The energy representation is remarkably robust here, and the contrast with the
Legendre-transformed potentials is instructive.

At a first-order transition the two phases $\ell$ and $g$ satisfy the coexistence
conditions
\[
T_\ell=T_g\equiv T_t,\qquad P_\ell=P_g\equiv P_t,\qquad \mu_\ell=\mu_g\equiv\mu_t ,
\]
which state that heat, volume and particles have nothing to gain by moving from
one phase to the other (Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}a). Read as a statement about
the function $U(S,V,N)$, these say that its three first partial derivatives take
the same values on either side. The first derivatives of $U$ are therefore
continuous, $U$ is $C^{1}$ across the transition, and nothing in
$dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN$ is in trouble.

At fixed $N$ and fixed $T_t$ on the coexistence curve, the two-phase states form
a straight segment in the $(S,V)$ plane --- the \emph{tie line} --- joining
$(S_\ell,V_\ell)$ to $(S_g,V_g)$: a mixture with liquid fraction $x$ has
$S=xS_\ell+(1-x)S_g$, $V=xV_\ell+(1-x)V_g$ and $U=xU_\ell+(1-x)U_g$, all three
interpolating linearly in the same $x$ (the lever rule; the interface costs
$O(L^{d-1})$ and is subextensive). Eliminating $x$ shows that $U$ is an
\emph{affine}\footnote{\label{fn:affine}\textbf{Affine function.} A linear
function plus a constant: $f(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x}+b$. Its graph
is a straight line, plane or hyperplane, and all its second derivatives vanish;
it is both convex and concave, but not strictly either
(footnote~\ref{fn:convex}).} function of $(S,V)$ along each tie line, so the
two-phase part of the $U$ surface is swept out by straight segments as $T_t$ runs
along the coexistence curve: a \emph{ruled} surface, flat along each tie line and
curved transverse to them.

Parametrise a tie line by $\lambda$, with $\lambda=0$ at the liquid end and
$\lambda=1$ at the gas end, and extend it into the single-phase regions
(Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}b). Then $U(\lambda)$ is convex and curved outside
$[0,1]$ and exactly straight on it. On the straight piece $T$ and $P$ are
\emph{constant} --- constant, not discontinuous --- being the slopes of an affine
function, and their values are the single-phase slopes at the endpoints, so the
flat piece joins the curved pieces with a common tangent. That is the whole
content of the statement that the two phases share a tangent plane, and it is the
double-tangent construction of Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell} read in the energy
representation.

What $U$ loses is therefore not differentiability but \emph{strict}
convexity.\footnote{\label{fn:convex}\textbf{Convexity.} A function $f$ on a
convex domain is \emph{convex} if
$f\big(x\lambda_1+(1-x)\lambda_2\big)\leq xf(\lambda_1)+(1-x)f(\lambda_2)$ for
all $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ in the domain and all $x\in[0,1]$: the chord never lies
below the graph. It is \emph{strictly convex} if the inequality is strict
whenever $\lambda_1\neq\lambda_2$ and $0<x<1$, which excludes any straight
segment in the graph. \emph{Concave} and \emph{strictly concave} mean the same
with the inequalities reversed, equivalently $-f$ convex. For $f\in C^{1}$,
convexity is equivalent to $f'$ non-decreasing and strict convexity to $f'$
strictly increasing; for $f\in C^{2}$, to $f''\geq0$, while $f''>0$ is sufficient
but not necessary for strictness ($\lambda^{4}$ is strictly convex with
$f''(0)=0$). An affine function is convex and concave but strictly neither.} A convex function that is affine on a segment is still
$C^{1}$ --- the slope is continuous, it simply stops changing --- but its second
derivative vanishes on the segment and jumps at the endpoints, so $U$ is $C^{1}$
and not $C^{2}$. Since $C_V$, $\kappa_T$ and every other response function is
built from second derivatives, they are what misbehave; the one-form $dU$ is
untouched:
\[
dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN \qquad\text{survives a first-order transition intact.}
\]

This is where the apparent paradox of a ``jump in $S$ and $V$ with $U$ still
$C^{1}$'' resolves. Nothing jumps as a function of $(S,V,N)$: $S$ and $V$ are the
\emph{coordinates} of $U$, and $U$ is an ordinary $C^{1}$ function of them
everywhere. What jumps is $S$ and $V$ regarded as functions of $(T,P)$ --- the
\emph{inverse} map. Since $T$ and $P$ are constant along the whole tie line, the
map $(S,V)\mapsto(T,P)$ collapses a segment to a single point, so its inverse
cannot be continuous: as $T$ crosses $T_t$ at fixed $P$, the realised state jumps
from one end of the segment to the other. A jump in $S(T,P)$ and a $C^{1}$
function $U(S,V)$ are two readings of one non-invertibility.

Read in the transformed potential, this is Ehrenfest's classification
(Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}c,d). Because $S$ and $V$ jump, $G(T,P,N)$ is
continuous but not $C^{1}$, and $dG=-S\,dT+V\,dP+\mu\,dN$ is invalid \emph{on}
the transition line though valid on either side. Ehrenfest classifies by the
lowest discontinuous derivative of the potential in the \emph{controlled}
variables, which in the laboratory are $(T,P)$: a transition is first order
exactly when the first derivatives of $G$ jump, i.e.\ exactly when (A4) fails for
$G$. At a continuous transition $G$ is $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$, so $dG$ survives
while the response functions diverge (Section~\ref{sec:critical}). The two
statements --- $U$ is $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$, $G$ is $C^{0}$ but not $C^{1}$ ---
are Legendre duals, not competing claims. The fundamental relation in extensive
variables is thus the most robust representation available, and each Legendre
transform trades some of that robustness for an experimentally controllable
variable.

across it (red arrow) even though $S$ and $V$ jump; $C$ is the critical point.
