single-phase branches by a common tangent (grey dashed): only the curvature
jumps, so $U$ is $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$. \textbf{(c)} At fixed $P$ the physical
$G$ is the lower of the two branches (solid; dashed continuations are
metastable), so $G(T)$ acquires a kink at $T_t$. \textbf{(d)} The kink means
$S=-(\partial G/\partial T)_{P,N}$ jumps by the latent-heat entropy, so $dG$ is
meaningless \emph{on} the transition line though valid on either side. Panels (b)
and (c)--(d) are one object seen through conjugate variables.}
\label{fig:firstorder}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Geometric restatement}

Fix a point $p$ of the state manifold $\mathcal{M}$. The \emph{tangent space}
$T_p\mathcal{M}$ is the space of infinitesimal displacements at $p$: in the chart
$(S,V,N)$ a tangent vector is a triple of rates $X=(\dot S,\dot V,\dot N)$, the
velocity of a curve through $p$. A \emph{covector} is an element of the dual
space $T^{*}_p\mathcal{M}$ --- a machine that eats a displacement and returns a
number, linearly --- and a \emph{one-form} is a choice of covector at every
point. For a $C^{1}$ function $f$, its \emph{differential} $df$ is the one-form
whose value on $X$ is the rate of change of $f$ along $X$; in the chart,
\[
df_p(X)=\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S}\right)_p\dot S
+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial V}\right)_p\dot V
+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial N}\right)_p\dot N .
\]
The map $f\mapsto df$ is the \emph{exterior derivative}, written $d$; on functions
it is nothing more than the operation just displayed. Applied to the coordinates
it gives $dS_p(X)=\dot S$ and so on, so $\{dS_p,dV_p,dN_p\}$ is a basis of the
three-dimensional space $T^{*}_p\mathcal{M}$.

The boxed relation $dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN$ is therefore an identity between
covectors: an exact equality of linear maps at each point, not an approximation
and not a statement about ``small'' quantities. Its coefficients are unique
because $\{dS,dV,dN\}$ is a basis, which is (A3), and it holds pointwise because
$U$ is $C^{1}$, which is (A4).

$d$ applies to every $C^{1}$ function on $\mathcal{M}$, whether or not it is a
coordinate of the chart in use: $T=(\partial U/\partial S)_{V,N}$ is such a
function, so $dT$ exists in exactly the same sense as $dS$, with an expansion in
$\{dS,dV,dN\}$ whose coefficients are the second derivatives of $U$ (so this
requires $U\in C^{2}$). Which covectors happen to be basis elements depends on
the chart --- in $(T,V,N)$ it is $dT$ that is one and $dS$ that is not --- and
neither statement bears on the existence of either object.
Appendix~\ref{app:basis} spells out why uniqueness of an expansion is a property
of a basis and only of a basis, which is what the intensive triple violates.

This viewpoint also sharpens Appendix~\ref{app:exact}: $\dbar Q$ is a perfectly
respectable one-form; what it lacks is being the differential \emph{of} anything
--- a distinction unavailable in the language of ``small quantities'', and the
reason for the notation.

\subsection{Extensivity: Euler and Gibbs--Duhem}

$U$, $S$, $V$, $N$ are extensive, so for $\lambda>0$
\[
U(\lambda S,\lambda V,\lambda N)=\lambda\, U(S,V,N).
\]
Differentiating with respect to $\lambda$ at $\lambda=1$ gives the \emph{Euler
relation}
\[
\boxed{\;U = TS - PV + \mu N\;}
\]

\paragraph{The Gibbs--Duhem relation.} Take the differential of Euler's relation
and subtract the fundamental relation,
\[
\underbrace{S\,dT+T\,dS}_{d(TS)}
\;-\;\underbrace{\big(P\,dV+V\,dP\big)}_{d(PV)}
\;+\;\underbrace{\mu\,dN+N\,d\mu}_{d(\mu N)}
\;=\;T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN ,
\]
whence
\[
\boxed{\;S\,dT - V\,dP + N\,d\mu = 0\;}
\]
Two steps deserve a word. The product rule $d(fg)=f\,dg+g\,df$ holds for any two
$C^{1}$ functions on $\mathcal{M}$, coordinates or not: in a chart both sides
have $i$-th component $f\,\partial_ig+g\,\partial_if$, and holding in every chart
it is chart independent. And $S\,dT$ means the one-form $dT$ rescaled pointwise
by the number $S(p)$. Since $T=U_S$, this presupposes $U\in C^{2}$ --- exactly
the differentiability that fails at a first-order transition, where Gibbs--Duhem
accordingly ceases to be differentiable along the coexistence line while
continuing to hold on either side.

Euler's relation is an identity between functions, so $d$ turns it into one
between one-forms: Gibbs--Duhem says the single one-form $S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu$
vanishes at every point. Expanding $dT,dP,d\mu$ in the basis $\{dS,dV,dN\}$ and
setting the three coefficients to zero gives three identities among second
derivatives of $U$; writing $x=(S,V,N)$,
\[
\sum_{j}x_j\,\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial x_i\,\partial x_j}=0
\qquad (i=1,2,3),
\]
i.e.\ the Hessian of $U$ is singular with null vector $(S,V,N)$. This is easily
confused with the Maxwell relations and is a different fact: Maxwell expresses
the \emph{symmetry} of the Hessian and follows from $U\in C^{2}$, whereas this
expresses its \emph{degeneracy} and follows from extensivity. The degeneracy is
why the response functions of a simple system are not all independent --- one
combination is fixed by homogeneity alone. Read instead as a statement about
independence, Gibbs--Duhem says $dT,dP,d\mu$ satisfy one linear relation at every
point, with a coefficient vector $(S,-V,N)$ that never vanishes: they span a
two-dimensional subspace of a three-dimensional cotangent space and cannot be a
basis, so fixing two of $T,P,\mu$ fixes the third. That is the failure of (A3)
for the intensive triple.

\paragraph{Absence of a potential $\Lambda(T,P,\mu)$.}
\label{sec:nofourth}
Trading all three extensive variables at once would give
\[
\Lambda\;\equiv\;U-TS+PV-\mu N\;\overset{\text{Euler}}{=}\;0
\]
\emph{identically} --- not zero at some special point, but the zero function on
$\mathcal{M}$. A function that is identically zero determines nothing, and its
differential is no help either: $d\Lambda=0$ is Gibbs--Duhem again.

A dimensional argument needs no algebra: $\Lambda$ is a sum of extensive
quantities, hence extensive, but was constructed as a function of $(T,P,\mu)$
alone, which scaling leaves unchanged --- so $\lambda\Lambda=\Lambda$ for all
$\lambda>0$, forcing $\Lambda\equiv0$. The same argument shows why $\Omega$ and
$G$ survive: each keeps one extensive variable ($V$ and $N$) to carry the
scaling.

\paragraph{Two consistency identities.} Substituting Euler's relation into the
definitions gives
\[
\boxed{\;\Omega = -PV, \qquad G = \mu N\;}
\]
and likewise $F=-PV+\mu N$, $H=TS+\mu N$. Each is a nontrivial constraint:
$\Omega(T,V,\mu)$ is not merely some function but $V$ times a function of
$(T,\mu)$ alone --- Euler homogeneity in the only extensive variable $\Omega$ has
left. Writing $\Omega=-V\pi(T,\mu)$ gives
$-(\partial\Omega/\partial V)_{T,\mu}=\pi$, so $\pi=P$ and $P$ is a function of
$(T,\mu)$ only, which is Gibbs--Duhem once more.

All of this assumes strict extensivity. Appendix~\ref{app:extensivity} states the
criterion and the three regimes in which it holds, is nearly exact, or fails
outright.

\subsection{Criticality: extensivity without analyticity}
\label{sec:critical}

Two properties are easy to conflate, and exactly one of them fails at a critical
point.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Euler homogeneity} is scaling in the \emph{extensive} variables at
fixed intensive ones: $u=Ts-P+\mu n$ for the densities, i.e.\ doubling the sample
doubles $U$ and $S$. It requires only short-range interactions and a correlation
length small compared with the sample. It holds at and arbitrarily close to a
critical point.
\item \textbf{Second-order smoothness} of the potential in its \emph{intensive}
variables. This is what fails, and with it analyticity.\footnote{\label{fn:analytic}\textbf{Analytic
versus differentiable.} $f$ is \emph{analytic} at $x_0$ if its Taylor series
about $x_0$ converges to $f$ in some neighbourhood of $x_0$. This is strictly
stronger than $C^{\infty}$, which only requires all derivatives to exist and be
continuous: $e^{-1/t^{2}}$ (extended by $0$ at $t=0$) is $C^{\infty}$ with every
derivative vanishing at the origin, so its Taylor series is identically zero and
represents the function nowhere but at $t=0$. \emph{Non-analytic} at $x_0$ thus covers three
situations, and it is worth knowing which is meant: $f$ may fail to be
differentiable (a kink, as in $G$ at a first-order transition); it may be $C^{k}$
but not $C^{k+1}$ (as in $f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{2-\alpha}$ at a continuous
transition, $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$ for $0<\alpha<1$); or it may be $C^{\infty}$
with a Taylor series that diverges or misrepresents it. Phase transitions realise
the first two. Only analyticity, not differentiability, is what a finite
partition function guarantees and what the thermodynamic limit destroys.}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The two-dimensional Ising specific heat.} Onsager's exact solution
gives a free energy density whose singular part behaves, with $t=(T-T_c)/T_c$, as
\[
f_{\rm sing}(t)\;\sim\; A\,t^{2}\ln|t| ,\qquad A>0 .
\]
Two bookkeeping points before differentiating.

\emph{Which specific heat.} For a lattice spin model the natural variables are
$(T,h)$ with $h$ the field, and $m$ is conjugate to $h$ exactly as $n$ is to
$\mu$, so $f(T,h)$ is the lattice analogue of the grand potential density
$\omega(T,\mu)$, not of the Helmholtz density $f(T,n)$. Hence
$c\equiv c_h=-T(\partial^{2}f/\partial T^{2})_{h}$ is the specific heat at fixed
\emph{field}, the analogue of $c_{V,\mu}$ and not of $c_{V,N}$. This is not
pedantry: as Appendix~\ref{app:fisher} shows, the fixed-$m$ and fixed-$h$
specific heats carry different critical exponents whenever $\alpha>0$. All values
of $\alpha$ below are fixed-field values, as is conventional.

\emph{No singular derivative is dropped.} For $t\neq0$ --- the only place any
derivative is claimed --- $d\ln|t|/dt=1/t$ on both branches, so
$df_{\rm sing}/dt=A(2t\ln|t|+t)\to0$ and
$d^{2}f_{\rm sing}/dt^{2}=A(2\ln|t|+3)$: $f_{\rm sing}$ is $C^{1}$ at $t=0$ and
not $C^{2}$. Since $\partial^{2}/\partial T^{2}=T_c^{-2}\,d^{2}/dt^{2}$,
\[
c_{h}\;\longrightarrow\;-\tilde A\ln|t|+\text{const},
\qquad \tilde A=\frac{2A}{T_c}>0 ,
\]
which diverges to $+\infty$ because $\ln|t|\to-\infty$. The two-dimensional Ising
specific heat therefore diverges \emph{logarithmically}, not as a power law; in
the classification below this is the marginal case $\alpha=0$.

The same remark disposes of the general power law: for $p>0$ and $t\neq0$,
$d^{2}|t|^{p}/dt^{2}=p(p-1)|t|^{p-2}$, the two sign functions cancelling at
second order, so the singularity sits entirely in the prefactor and appears only
as $t\to0$. A genuine $\delta$-function would arise only from a term with a
discontinuous \emph{value}, such as $\operatorname{sgn}(t)$ --- which is what the
order parameter does at a first-order transition, and why $dG$ fails there and
$dU$ does not.

Throughout, extensivity remains exact: $F=Vf$, $\Omega=-PV$, Gibbs--Duhem holds.
But $f(t)$ has no Taylor series about $t=0$, and the specific heat --- an
innocuous second derivative of a perfectly extensive potential --- diverges.

\paragraph{The scaling form and the gap exponent.} Near a critical point the
singular part of the free energy density takes the \emph{generalized homogeneous}
form
\[
f_{\rm sing}(t,h)=|t|^{2-\alpha}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\Delta}}\right),
\qquad
c_{h}\sim|t|^{-\alpha}\quad(h=0),
\]
where $\Delta$ is the \emph{gap exponent}, defined by the requirement that
$h/|t|^{\Delta}$ be the scale-invariant combination, and $\Phi$ is a
\emph{universal scaling function}: a function determined by the universality
class alone --- dimensionality, order-parameter symmetry, interaction range ---
and not by microscopic details. Universality is a statement about $\Phi$ up to
two non-universal scale factors: for two systems in the same class,
\[
f_{\rm sing}(t,h)=A_0\,|t|^{2-\alpha}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{B_0h}{|t|^{\Delta}}\right)
\]
with the \emph{same} function $\Phi$ and only $A_0$, $B_0$, and the location of
$T_c$ differing. $\Phi$ is analytic on each branch $t>0$, $t<0$ away from the
origin; the scaling hypothesis is precisely the assumption that all the
non-analyticity has been collected into the prefactor and the argument.

Each field derivative lowers the exponent of $|t|$ by the same \emph{gap}
$\Delta$,
\[
\frac{\partial^{k}f_{\rm sing}}{\partial h^{k}}\sim|t|^{2-\alpha-k\Delta},
\]
so $k=1$ gives $m\sim|t|^{\beta}$ and $k=2$ gives $\chi\sim|t|^{-\gamma}$, whence
\[
\boxed{\;\Delta=\beta\delta=\beta+\gamma\;}
\]
and eliminating $\Delta$ between the two is the Rushbrooke identity
$\alpha+2\beta+\gamma=2$. For the three-dimensional Ising class
$\beta\simeq0.326$ and $\gamma\simeq1.237$, so $\Delta\simeq1.563$. This
homogeneity is of a different kind from Euler's: Euler is degree-one homogeneity
in $(S,V,N)$ with integer exponent, this is homogeneity in $(t,h)$ with
non-integer exponents.

Appendix~\ref{app:cusp} works out what the sign of $\alpha$ does to the shape of
$c_h$, and what universality does and does not deliver in practice.

\begin{remark}[Origin of the non-analyticity]
For any \emph{finite} system, $Z=\sum_ie^{-\beta E_i}$ is a finite sum of
positive exponentials, hence an entire function of $\beta$ with no zeros on the
real axis, so $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is analytic and no phase transition can occur.
Non-analyticity is generated only in the thermodynamic limit, when the complex
zeros of $Z$ (Yang--Lee zeros in the fugacity plane, Fisher zeros in the
temperature plane) pinch the real axis. A phase transition is a property of the
limit, never of a finite sample --- which is precisely why finite-size scaling is
needed to extract critical behaviour from simulation (Section~\ref{sec:fss}).
\end{remark}

\section{Heat and work}
\label{sec:heat}

\subsection{Derivation of \texorpdfstring{$\dbar Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS$}{dQrev = T dS}}

This relation is the hinge on which the rest of these notes turn, and it follows
from the Gibbs state alone. Take $\rho$ Gibbsian with $S=-k_B\sum_ip_i\ln p_i$,
and let $\lambda$ stand for the external parameters the experimenter controls
(volume, field, strain), work being by definition the energy delivered through
$\lambda$. Substituting $\ln p_i=-\beta E_i-\ln Z$ gives the exact identity
$S=k_B(\ln Z+\beta U)$. Differentiate, using $d\ln Z=-U\,d\beta-\beta\,\dbar W$,
which follows from $\ln Z$ depending on $\beta$ and on $\lambda$ through the
$E_i$:
\[
\frac{dS}{k_B}=d\ln Z+U\,d\beta+\beta\,dU
=-U d\beta-\beta\dbar W+U d\beta+\beta\,dU
=\beta\big(dU-\dbar W\big),
\]
that is,
\[
\boxed{\;T\,dS=dU-\dbar W=\dbar Q_{\rm rev}\;}
\]
The only inputs are that the state is Gibbsian and that work is the energy
delivered through $\lambda$. Heat is the energy transfer not accounted for by the
controlled parameters, entropy measures the spread of the distribution, and $T$
is the exchange rate between them.

\subsection{Quasi-static versus reversible}
\label{sec:clausius}

\paragraph{The Clausius inequality.} The second law states that for a thermally
isolated composite system taken from one equilibrium state to another,
$\Delta S_{\rm tot}\geq0$, with equality if and only if the process is
reversible.

The statement is written with $\Delta S_{\rm tot}$ rather than $dS_{\rm tot}$
deliberately. Entropy is defined for \emph{equilibrium} states, so
$dS_{\rm tot}$ presumes the system has an entropy at every instant --- false for
a violently irreversible process: while a gas rushes into a vacuum there is no
equilibrium state and no entropy there to differentiate. All the second law needs
is that $S$ be defined at the initial and final points, which are equilibrium
states by hypothesis.

The differential form $dS_{\rm tot}\geq0$ is licensed in exactly two
circumstances. A process is \emph{quasi-static} if it is slow compared with all
internal relaxation times; every intermediate state is then an equilibrium state,
the path is a genuine curve on the equilibrium manifold, and $dS_{\rm tot}$ is a
one-form evaluated on that curve's tangent vector. Alternatively, under
\emph{local equilibrium} --- the system partitioned into cells each internally
equilibrated on the timescale of observation --- one may define an entropy
density and obtain the local statement $\partial_ts+\nabla\!\cdot\mathbf{J}_s
=\sigma\geq0$, with $\mathbf{J}_s$ the entropy current and $\sigma$ the local
production rate. This is an additional hypothesis, not a theorem; it is the
foundation of irreversible thermodynamics, and it fails in shock fronts, in
rarefied gases and in glasses.

The reservoir is by construction always internally equilibrated, so
$S_{\rm res}$ is defined at every instant and $dS_{\rm res}=-\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}$
carries no caveat. Eliminating it at the integrated level therefore needs no
quasi-static assumption at all: $\Delta S_{\rm res}=-\int\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}$ is
exact, and $\Delta S_{\rm tot}\geq0$ gives
\[
\boxed{\;\Delta S\;\geq\;\int\frac{\dbar Q}{T_{\rm res}}\;}
\]
between any two equilibrium states, however violent the path. If in addition the
process is quasi-static for the system, so that $dS$ is meaningful, the same
elimination gives the differential \emph{Clausius inequality}
\[
\boxed{\;dS \geq \frac{\dbar Q}{T_{\rm res}}\;}
\]
Clausius' statement is the second law with the reservoir's entropy eliminated,
and nothing else. Note that it compares the system's entropy change with the
\emph{reservoir's} temperature: a finite temperature drop across the boundary
already costs entropy even when both bodies are internally equilibrated. The same
elimination, carried out for each choice of constraint, produces the minimum
principles of Section~\ref{sec:minimum}. Naming the entropy produced
$d\sigma\equiv dS_{\rm tot}\geq0$ turns the inequality into the equality
$dS=\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}+d\sigma$, so that $T\,dS=\dbar Q+T\,d\sigma\geq\dbar Q$
when $T=T_{\rm res}$, with equality if and only if $d\sigma=0$.

\paragraph{Why the two words differ.} \emph{Reversible} means quasi-static
\emph{and} dissipationless: no friction, no finite temperature or pressure drops
across the boundary, no hysteresis, no chemical reaction running downhill. So
quasi-staticity is necessary for $\dbar Q=T\,dS$ and not sufficient. Standard
counterexamples, all arbitrarily slow yet all with $\dbar Q<T\,dS$:

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Joule heating.} Stir a thermally isolated fluid infinitesimally
slowly. The fluid stays in equilibrium, so the process is quasi-static;
$\dbar Q=0$ because the vessel is adiabatic; yet $dS>0$, since the work is
degraded into internal energy. Here $T\,dS=T\,d\sigma$ entirely.
\item \emph{Heat flow across a finite $\Delta T$.} If $T_{\rm res}\neq T$ then
$\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}<\dbar Q/T=dS$ for heating. The system alone is quasi-static;
the composite is not. Compression against sliding friction is of the same kind.
\item \emph{Hysteretic systems.} Domain-wall depinning (Barkhausen noise),
martensitic transformations and vortex-lattice motion all dissipate a finite work
per cycle however slowly the field is swept: the $\omega\to0$ limit of the loop
area is nonzero, so the quasi-static limit is genuinely irreversible.
\item \emph{Glasses and slow variables.} If some internal variable has
$\tau_\xi\sim\tau_{\rm obs}$ the system is never truly equilibrated, which is why
the specific heat of a glass former is frequency-dependent,
$c(\omega)=c'(\omega)-ic''(\omega)$, with $c''$ measuring exactly the dissipation
that spoils $\dbar Q=T\,dS$. Appendix~\ref{app:glass} sets out which parts of the
apparatus survive in that case.
\end{itemize}

\begin{remark}[Robustness of $c_V$]
At fixed volume and particle number no work is done, so $\dbar Q=dU$
\emph{regardless of reversibility}. Hence $C_V=(\partial U/\partial T)_{V,N}$
holds even for irreversible heating, which is why constant-volume calorimetry is
well behaved. The step $\dbar Q=T\,dS$ is needed only to re-express this as
$C_V=T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,N}$ and connect it to the free energy. For
$C_P$, or any process involving mechanical work, the reversibility caveat bites
in earnest.
\end{remark}

The exactness criterion for one-forms, the Maxwell relations it generates, and
the sense in which $1/T$ is an integrating factor are collected in
Appendix~\ref{app:exact}.

\section{Thermodynamic potentials}
\label{sec:potentials}

An experiment fixes intensive variables: a sample sits in a bath at temperature
$T$, not at prescribed entropy. The obvious move is naive substitution --- invert
$T=(\partial U/\partial S)_{V,N}$ to get $S(T,V,N)$ and define
$\tilde U(T,V,N)\equiv U\big(S(T,V,N),V,N\big)$. This destroys information; the
Legendre transform does not. Two questions must be separated: whether the
inversion is possible, and whether the substitution keeps what it should.

\subsection{Invertibility and strict convexity}

Nonlinearity of $T(S)$ is not an obstacle. What a function needs in order to be
invertible is \emph{strict monotonicity}, and strict convexity of $U$ in $S$
delivers exactly that. The implication is one line: for $S_1<S_2$, strict
convexity (footnote~\ref{fn:convex}) gives both
\[
U(S_2)-U(S_1)>U'(S_1)(S_2-S_1)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
U(S_1)-U(S_2)>U'(S_2)(S_1-S_2),
\]
since a strictly convex function lies strictly above each of its tangent lines
away from the point of tangency. Adding the two and dividing by $S_2-S_1>0$
gives
\[
U'(S_2)-U'(S_1)>0,
\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad T(S_2)>T(S_1) .
\]
So $T(S)$ is strictly increasing, hence injective, and the inverse function
theorem supplies a $C^{1}$ inverse $S(T,V,N)$ on the whole stable branch. In
differential form the same statement is
\[
\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial S}\right)_{V,N}
=\left(\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial S^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=\frac{T}{C_V}>0
\qquad\text{whenever } C_V>0 ,
\]
and Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex} shows that $C_V>0$ is not an assumption but a
consequence of the second law. Nonlinearity is irrelevant to this:
$T=\alpha S^{3}+\beta e^{S}$ with $\alpha,\beta>0$ is thoroughly nonlinear and
thoroughly invertible.

Invertibility fails only where \emph{strict} convexity does. At a first-order
transition $U$ is affine in $S$ along a tie line (Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}b),
so $C_V=\infty$ and $(\partial T/\partial S)_{V,N}=0$: an entire interval of $S$
maps to the single value $T_t$, and $S(T)$ does not exist as a function. This is
the genuine breakdown, repaired by the Legendre--Fenchel transform of
Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}. Inside a spinodal region a \emph{computed} mean-field
$U$ can be concave, making $T(S)$ non-monotonic; the exact $U$ is never concave
and such states are not physical.

\subsection{What naive substitution loses}

On the stable branch the inversion is sound, and the naive substitution is a
disaster anyway.

\paragraph{The loss is a whole function.} What goes missing is not a constant ---
which a statement about the domain of $\tilde U$ might still recover --- but a
function of the remaining variables, and nothing outside $\tilde U$ can supply
it. For the classical monatomic ideal
gas the energy is volume-independent, $U=\tfrac32Nk_BT$, so
$\tilde U(T,V,N)=\tfrac32Nk_BT$ knows nothing about $V$: it cannot produce the
equation of state, since $-(\partial\tilde U/\partial V)$ is identically zero
rather than $Nk_BT/V$; it cannot distinguish an ideal gas from any other
substance whose energy happens not to depend on volume; and no restriction of its
domain repairs the deficit, because the missing object is a function of $V$. The
Helmholtz free energy,
\[
F(T,V,N)=-Nk_BT\left[\ln\frac{V}{N\lambda_T^{3}}+1\right],
\]
returns $P=-(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N}=Nk_BT/V$ and the Sackur--Tetrode
entropy $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$ at once.

\paragraph{What is lost: a function of integration.} Since $\tilde U=U$ evaluated
at $S=S(T,V,N)$, and $F=U-TS$ with $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$,
\[
\boxed{\;\tilde U=F-T\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}\;}
\]
Getting $\tilde U$ from $F$ is a \emph{differentiation}. Going the other way
means solving, at each fixed $(V,N)$, the linear first-order equation
$\partial_TF-F/T=-\tilde U/T$, whose homogeneous solution is $F_{\rm hom}=C\,T$
with $C$ constant in $T$ but an arbitrary function of the other variables. Hence
$F=F_{\rm part}(T,V,N)+C(V,N)\,T$, and $\tilde U$ fixes $F$ --- equivalently $U$
--- only up to $C(V,N)$. In one variable $C$ degenerates to a number, which is
why the toy example could be rescued by a domain argument; in the physical case
it is an entire function, and nothing outside $\tilde U$ can supply it.

The ambiguity is physical, not a gauge. Adding $C(V,N)T$ to $F$ shifts $S$ by
$-C(V,N)$ at each $(V,N)$ and leaves $U$, viewed as a function of $(T,V,N)$,
unchanged --- which is why $\tilde U$ cannot see it. In the energy representation
the family is $U_C(S,V,N)\equiv U\big(S+C(V,N),V,N\big)$, whose members have
different pressures, $P_C=P-T\,\partial C/\partial V$. Applied to the ideal gas
with $C=Nk_B\ln(V/V_0)$ this gives $P_C\equiv0$: a formally admissible
fundamental relation $U_C(S,N)=c\,N^{5/3}V_0^{-2/3}e^{2S/3Nk_B}$, describing a
substance with no mechanical equation of state at all, and with
$\tilde U_C=\tfrac32Nk_BT$ identical to the gas's. Two materials that different
share the same $\tilde U$; they do not share the same $F$.

\paragraph{The asymmetry between the two directions.} Recovering $U$ from $F$
requires no integration: $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$ is a
differentiation, and $U=F+TS$ then follows algebraically, with only the inversion
$S\leftrightarrow T$ --- guaranteed by $C_V>0$ --- needed to express the result
in $(S,V,N)$. Differentiating introduces no constants of integration;
integrating does. That asymmetry, and not any subtlety about invertibility, is
why $F$ is a potential and $\tilde U$ is merely a function.

\subsection{Geometric content: envelope of tangents}

The Legendre transform works because a convex function can be described in two
equivalent ways: as the set of points $\big(S,U(S)\big)$, or as the envelope of
its tangent lines. The tangent at $S_0$ has slope $T=U'(S_0)$ and satisfies
\[
y=U(S_0)+T(S-S_0)=T\,S+\underbrace{\big[U(S_0)-TS_0\big]}_{=\,F(T)} ,
\]
so $F(T)$ is the \emph{intercept} of the tangent of slope $T$. Specifying all
(slope, intercept) pairs specifies the envelope, hence $U(S)$: no information is
lost. Naive substitution keeps only the slopes and discards the intercepts.

\subsection{Convexity of \texorpdfstring{$U$}{U} in \texorpdfstring{$S$}{S}}
\label{sec:whyconvex}

Convexity is not an assumption; it follows from the second law. Divide a system
into two equal halves that may exchange energy. At fixed total energy $2E$ the
total entropy is maximal at the actual equilibrium, which by symmetry is equal
division, so $S(E_1)+S(E_2)\leq2S\big((E_1+E_2)/2\big)$ --- midpoint concavity,
which with continuity gives concavity of $S(E)$ outright. Since also $\partial
S/\partial E=1/T>0$, $S$ is concave and increasing, and the inverse of a concave
increasing function is convex increasing, so $U(S)$ is convex, i.e.\
$(\partial^{2}U/\partial S^{2})_{V,N}=T/C_V\geq0$. Convexity of $U$ in $S$,
positivity of $C_V$ and entropy maximisation of the composite are three
statements of one fact; Section~\ref{sec:convexity} turns the same construction
into the stability inequalities for every response function.

\subsection{Legendre versus Legendre--Fenchel}

The classical Legendre transform is defined by a stationarity condition and a
substitution:
\[
F(T)=U(S^\ast)-TS^\ast, \qquad S^\ast \text{ solving } \frac{\partial
U}{\partial S}=T ,
\]
and there is no minimisation in that definition. For convex differentiable $U$ it
coincides with the \emph{Legendre--Fenchel} transform,\footnote{\label{fn:inf}\textbf{$\inf$
and $\sup$.} For a set $A$ of real numbers, $\inf A$ is its greatest lower bound,
and $\inf_{S}g(S)$ means the greatest lower bound of the values $g$ takes on its
domain. The point of writing $\inf$ rather than $\min$ is that the infimum always
exists in $\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}$ whereas a minimum need not be attained:
$e^{-S}$ on $[0,\infty)$ has $\inf=0$ and no minimum. For a strictly convex $g$
tending to $+\infty$ at the ends of its domain the infimum is attained and the
two coincide, which is the situation throughout this section. The value
$-\infty$ is not idle: $\inf_S[U(S)-TS]=-\infty$ is how the variational form
reports that $T$ lies outside the range of $\partial U/\partial S$, i.e.\ that no
state of the system has that temperature. The mirror notion $\sup$ is the least
upper bound.}
\[
\boxed{\;F(T)=\inf_{S}\big[\,U(S)-TS\,\big]\;}
\]
because for convex $U$ the function $S\mapsto U(S)-TS$ is convex and its unique
stationary point is its global minimum. The variational form is the more general
of the two: it is well defined for non-differentiable and even non-convex $U$,
and applying it twice returns not $U$ but its convex hull,
$U^{\ast\ast}=\operatorname{conv}U$ --- which is the Maxwell construction
(Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}).

The variational form is also the bridge to thermodynamics. In $\inf_S[U(S)-TS]$
the variable $S$ plays the role of an unconstrained internal variable: a system
coupled to a bath is free to exchange energy and thereby to choose its entropy,
and it settles at the value minimising $U-TS$, the stationarity condition
$\partial U/\partial S=T$ being the statement that system and bath have reached a
common temperature. The minimum principle of Section~\ref{sec:minimum} and the
Legendre--Fenchel form are the same equation read in two directions; neither is
the same as the bare definition of the transform.

\subsection{The family}

\paragraph{Internal energy.} Natural variables $(S,V,N)$:
\[
\boxed{\;dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN\;}
\]

\paragraph{Enthalpy.}
\[
\boxed{\;H=U+PV\;},\qquad
\boxed{\;dH=T\,dS+V\,dP+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
so $H=H(S,P,N)$. At constant $P$ and $N$, $dH=\dbar Q_{\rm rev}$, which is why
enthalpy is the bookkeeping variable for calorimetry at atmospheric pressure and
gives $C_P=(\partial H/\partial T)_{P,N}$.

\paragraph{Helmholtz free energy.}
\[
\boxed{\;F=U-TS\;},\qquad
\boxed{\;dF=-S\,dT-P\,dV+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
so $F=F(T,V,N)$, with $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$,
$P=-(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N}$, $\mu=(\partial F/\partial N)_{T,V}$.

\paragraph{Gibbs free energy.}
\[
\boxed{\;G=U-TS+PV=F+PV\;},\qquad
\boxed{\;dG=-S\,dT+V\,dP+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
so $G=G(T,P,N)$, with $S=-(\partial G/\partial T)_{P,N}$ and $V=(\partial
G/\partial P)_{T,N}$. Since $G=\mu N$, the chemical potential is the Gibbs free
energy per particle.

\paragraph{Grand potential.}
\[
\boxed{\;\Omega=U-TS-\mu N=F-\mu N\;},\qquad
\boxed{\;d\Omega=-S\,dT-P\,dV-N\,d\mu\;}
\]
so $\Omega=\Omega(T,V,\mu)$, with $S=-(\partial\Omega/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$,
$P=-(\partial\Omega/\partial V)_{T,\mu}$, $N=-(\partial\Omega/\partial\mu)_{T,V}$.

The grand potential is indispensable for both quantum statistics, for the same
technical reason in each case: only in the grand ensemble does the many-body
problem factorise mode by mode, $\Xi=\prod_{k}\xi_{k}$, because the constraint
$\sum_kn_k=N$ that couples the modes has been traded for a Lagrange multiplier.
The Fermi--Dirac and Bose--Einstein distributions both emerge from this
factorisation and are essentially intractable at fixed $N$. Typical settings:
electrons exchanging with leads or with a gate that holds $\mu$, and any
Landauer-type transport problem with $\mu_L\neq\mu_R$; Bose--Einstein
condensation, defined by $\mu\to\varepsilon_0$; the Mott lobes of the
Bose--Hubbard model, whose flat tops at integer filling exist only because $\mu$
is the control variable; and trapped cold atoms, where the local density
approximation treats each region as grand-canonical with $\mu_{\rm
loc}=\mu-V_{\rm trap}(r)$. Photons and phonons have no conservation law at all,
so $\mu=0$ identically and the grand and canonical descriptions coincide.

\subsection{The minimum principle}
\label{sec:minimum}

\begin{quote}
At fixed values of its natural variables, the appropriate thermodynamic potential
is minimised with respect to all remaining unconstrained internal degrees of
freedom.
\end{quote}

``Internal degrees of freedom'' is used for three different things, of which only
one is meant. \emph{Microscopic} degrees of freedom --- atomic positions and
momenta, or the quantum many-body state --- are not minimised over; they are
summed over, traced out by the partition function, and the minimum principle sits
at a level of description from which they have disappeared. The \emph{natural
variables} of the potential are not varied either: they are what the walls and
reservoirs hold fixed, and minimising $F$ ``over $T$'' is meaningless. What one
minimises over is the \emph{macroscopic internal variables}: the coarse-grained
collective quantities left undetermined by the constraints.

These are exactly the $\xi$-type variables of Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}:
macroscopic averages, sharp and non-fluctuating in the thermodynamic limit, but
not pinned down by the imposed constraints --- the partitioning of a conserved
quantity between subsystems, the extent of a chemical reaction, the volume
fraction of a coexisting phase, an order parameter, the position of an internal
partition. The test for membership is operational: a variable belongs on the list
if the system can change it without violating any imposed constraint. Once it
can, the second law says the composite will change it in whichever direction
lowers the relevant potential, and equilibrium is where no such direction
remains.

\paragraph{Derivation.} The only fundamental extremum principle is the second
law: for an isolated composite, $S_{\rm tot}$ is maximised. Everything else
follows by eliminating the reservoir. With $dS_{\rm res}=-\dbar Q/T$,
\[
dS_{\rm tot}=dS-\frac{\dbar Q}{T}\geq 0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \dbar Q \leq T\,dS ,
\]
and inserting the first law $\dbar Q=dU-\dbar W$ for each set of constraints:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Held fixed & Second law becomes & Equilibrium condition\\
\midrule
$E,V,N$ (isolated) & $dS \geq 0$ & $S$ \emph{maximum}\\
$S,V,N$ & $dU \leq 0$ & $U$ minimum\\
$S,P,N$ & $dH \leq 0$ & $H$ minimum\\
$T,V,N$ & $dF \leq 0$ & $F$ minimum\\
$T,P,N$ & $dG \leq 0$ & $G$ minimum\\
$T,V,\mu$ & $d\Omega \leq 0$ & $\Omega$ minimum\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

For $T,V,N$ explicitly: at fixed $V$ no work is done, so $\dbar W=0$ and
$\dbar Q=dU$; the inequality $dU\leq T\,dS$ at fixed $T$ is
$d(U-TS)=dF\leq0$. The potential can only decrease, so it decreases until it
cannot: it is a Lyapunov function whose minimum is equilibrium. The sign flip
relative to entropy maximisation comes entirely from the reservoir's entropy
entering with the opposite sign.

\paragraph{The statistical-mechanical version.} For an arbitrary trial density
matrix $\rho$ define
\[
\mathcal{F}[\rho]=\Tr(\rho\hat H)+k_BT\,\Tr(\rho\ln\rho).
\]
Then $\mathcal{F}[\rho]-F=k_BT\,D(\rho\|\rho_{\rm eq})\geq0$, with equality only
for $\rho=\rho_{\rm eq}$. The equilibrium density matrix is the \emph{global}
minimiser of $\mathcal{F}$ and $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is the minimum value. This is the
Gibbs variational principle; the Gibbs--Bogoliubov inequality $F\leq
F_0+\langle\hat H-\hat H_0\rangle_0$, the workhorse of variational mean-field
theory, is the same statement with a restricted trial family.

\paragraph{The constrained free energy.} In practice one introduces an order
parameter $\phi$ and builds a constrained free energy $F(T,V,N;\phi)$. The
honest definition is a \emph{partial} trace. Let $\hat\phi$ be the operator whose
value is the order parameter, for instance
$\hat\phi=M^{-1}\sum_i\hat\sigma^z_i$ for magnetisation per site. Then
\[
\boxed{\;e^{-\beta F(T,V,N;\phi)}\;=\;\Tr\Big[\,\delta\big(\hat\phi-\phi\big)\,
e^{-\beta\hat H}\Big]\;}
\]
one sums the Boltzmann weight over only those microstates whose order parameter
has the prescribed value; nothing artificial is added, the constraint being a
restriction of the sum. Equivalently $F(\phi)=-k_BT\ln P(\phi)+\text{const}$ with
$P(\phi)$ the distribution of $\phi$ in the ordinary canonical ensemble, so
$F(\phi)$ is measurable and in a simulation is exactly what a histogram reports.
Summing over $\phi$ recovers the unconstrained free energy, and in the
thermodynamic limit that sum is dominated by its smallest term:
\[
F_{\rm eq}(T,V,N)=\min_{\phi}F(T,V,N;\phi)+O(\ln V),
\qquad
\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial \phi}\right|_{\phi_{\rm eq}}=0 .
\]

The conjugate-field route is a \emph{different} construction. Adding a source $h$
coupled to $\hat\phi$ gives an unconstrained $F_h=-k_BT\ln\Tr\,
e^{-\beta(\hat H-hV\hat\phi)}$, whose Legendre transform back to fixed $\phi$ is
$\hat F(\phi)=\min_h[F_h(h)+hV\phi]$ --- convex in $\phi$ by construction, so
$\hat F=\operatorname{conv}F$ and not $F$ itself. The two agree wherever $F$ is
already convex and differ exactly where it is not: below $T_c$ the field route
returns the flat common tangent between the two wells and is blind to the barrier
between them. Since that barrier is the whole content of nucleation,
metastability and hysteresis, the field construction cannot serve as the
definition: fixing the field performs the Maxwell construction automatically,
fixing the order parameter does not.

Two points make $F(\phi)$ concrete. At finite volume the double-well shape is
real, and its barrier height is the interfacial free energy of the domain wall
separating the two ordered regions, $\Delta F\sim2\sigma L^{d-1}$ --- the
Lee--Kosterlitz quantity of Section~\ref{sec:firstorder}, which diverges with $L$
and so survives the thermodynamic limit even though $F_{\rm eq}/V$ does not
remember it. And this $F(\phi)$ is the effective potential $\Gamma[\phi]$ of
field theory at constant $\phi$; that the Legendre route yields its convex hull
is the standard remark that a perturbatively computed $\Gamma$ can develop a
spurious imaginary part inside the non-convex region. Landau theory is the
expansion of $F(T;\phi)$ in powers of $\phi$, and the change in the location and
multiplicity of its minima as $T$ crosses $T_c$ \emph{is} the phase transition.

\begin{warning}
Stationarity alone is not equilibrium; one also needs stability, i.e.\ that the
extremum be a minimum. And a local minimum is a metastable state, not the
equilibrium state: supercooled liquids, retained austenite and diamond at ambient
conditions all sit in local minima with lifetimes exceeding any experiment.
Thermodynamics identifies the global minimum; whether the system reaches it is a
question of kinetics.
\end{warning}

Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell} treats what happens when the computed $F$ is
non-convex: the double-tangent construction, Maxwell's equal-area rule, and the
binodal/spinodal distinction.

\subsection{Convexity, stability, and response functions}
\label{sec:convexity}

\paragraph{Convexity flip under Legendre transform.} Read $F(T)=\inf_S[U(S)-TS]$
as a function of $T$ at fixed $S$: the bracket is affine in $T$, with slope $-S$
and intercept $U(S)$. So $F(T)$ is the pointwise infimum of a family of straight
lines, and a pointwise infimum of affine functions is concave --- draw two lines
and take the lower envelope --- regardless of the family:
\[
\boxed{\;F(T)=\inf_S\big[\underbrace{U(S)-TS}_{\text{affine in }T}\big]
\;\Longrightarrow\;F\ \text{concave in}\ T\;}
\]
Symmetrically $U(S)=\sup_T[F(T)+TS]$ is a supremum of affine functions of $S$ and
therefore convex, which is also why the double transform returns the convex hull
and nothing more. No differentiability is needed anywhere in this argument.

For smooth functions there is a differential version that says more. With
$T=U'(S)$ and $S=-F'(T)$,
\[
\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial T^{2}}=-\frac{dS}{dT}
=-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial S^{2}}\right)^{-1} .
\]
The curvatures are not merely opposite in sign but \emph{reciprocal}. Physically
this is a fluctuation statement: a potential very flat in $T$ (large $|F''|$,
large $C_V$) corresponds to a fundamental relation very stiff in $S$. A stiff
system has small fluctuations of the conjugate variable.

\paragraph{Stability as a second-order condition.} Every stability inequality
comes from one construction. Divide a homogeneous system conceptually into two
equal halves that may exchange a single extensive quantity $X$ through an
internal wall, the total $X_{\rm tot}=2X_0$ being held fixed from outside. The
split
\[
X_{1}=X_0+x,\qquad X_{2}=X_0-x
\]
is then an unconstrained internal variable in the sense of
Section~\ref{sec:minimum}, so the potential $\Phi$ selected by the remaining
constraints must be extremal at the homogeneous state $x=0$ --- and
\emph{stability is the demand that the extremum be a minimum} (a maximum, for
the entropy). Since
\[
\Phi_{\rm tot}(x)=\Phi(X_0+x)+\Phi(X_0-x),
\qquad
\Phi_{\rm tot}'(0)=\Phi'(X_0)-\Phi'(X_0)=0 ,
\qquad
\Phi_{\rm tot}''(0)=2\,\Phi''(X_0),
\]
the first-order condition holds identically by symmetry --- it says only that the
two halves share the intensive variable conjugate to $X$ --- and the entire
content is second order:
\[
\boxed{\;\Phi''(X_0)\;\geq\;0\quad\text{for a minimised potential},
\qquad S''(X_0)\;\leq\;0\quad\text{for the entropy}\;}
\]
Each choice of $X$ and of the internal wall gives one inequality, and in each
case the second derivative is a response function in disguise. Three cases,
worked out explicitly.

\paragraph{Heat capacity.} Take the composite \emph{isolated}, with a rigid,
impermeable, diathermal internal wall, so that the only free variable is the
energy split $X=E$ and the governing principle is maximum total entropy. The
construction gives $(\partial^{2}S/\partial E^{2})_{V,N}\leq0$ --- the argument
of Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex} carried to second order. Now evaluate that
derivative. From $(\partial S/\partial E)_{V,N}=1/T$ and
$(\partial E/\partial T)_{V,N}=C_V$,
\[
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial E^{2}}\right)_{V,N}
=\frac{\partial}{\partial E}\left(\frac{1}{T}\right)_{V,N}
=-\frac{1}{T^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial E}\right)_{V,N}
=-\frac{1}{T^{2}C_V}\;\leq\;0 ,
\]
and since $T>0$ the inequality forces the sign of $C_V$:
\[
\boxed{\;C_V\;\geq\;0\;}
\]
Read the failure mode directly off the algebra: $C_V<0$ would make
$\partial^{2}S/\partial E^{2}>0$, so $x=0$ would be a \emph{minimum} of
$S_{\rm tot}$ and any energy transfer would grow --- the half that gave energy
away would become the hotter of the two and go on giving. The statistical proof
is one line: $C_V=\Var(E)/k_BT^{2}$ by Section~\ref{sec:canonical}, and a
variance is non-negative.

\paragraph{Isothermal compressibility.} Now make the internal wall a movable,
diathermal, impermeable piston, hold the whole in a bath at temperature $T$, and
fix the total volume $2V_0$ and $N/2$ particles in each half. The constraints are
$(T,V,N)$, so the potential is $F$ and the free variable is $X=V$. The
construction gives $(\partial^{2}F/\partial V^{2})_{T,N}\geq0$; with
$(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N}=-P$ and
$\kappa_T\equiv-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_{T,N}$,
\[
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial V^{2}}\right)_{T,N}
=-\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
=\frac{1}{V\kappa_T}\;\geq\;0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;\kappa_T\;\geq\;0\;}
\]
Mechanically, $\kappa_T<0$ means $(\partial P/\partial V)_T>0$: a region that
contracted slightly would find its pressure \emph{fall} below that of its
surroundings and be compressed further. That runaway is exactly the interior of
the van der Waals loop, and phase separation is how the system escapes it
(Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}). Statistically, the isothermal--isobaric ensemble
gives $\Var(V)=k_BT\,V\kappa_T\geq0$.

\paragraph{Magnetic susceptibility.} Repeat with $X=M$: fix $T$, $V$, $N$ and the
total moment $2M_0$, and let the halves exchange moment. The potential at fixed
$M$ is the constrained free energy $F(T,V,N;M)$ of Section~\ref{sec:minimum},
whose derivative is the field, $(\partial F/\partial M)_{T,V,N}=\mathcal{H}$, so
\[
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial M^{2}}\right)_{T,V,N}
=\left(\frac{\partial \mathcal{H}}{\partial M}\right)_{T,V,N}
=\frac{1}{\chi_T}\;\geq\;0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;\chi_T\equiv\left(\frac{\partial M}{\partial\mathcal{H}}\right)_{T,V,N}
\;\geq\;0\;}
\]
Equivalently, in the field-controlled potential $\tilde F=F-\mathcal{H}M$
(Appendix~\ref{app:bookkeeping}) concavity in $\mathcal{H}$ reads
$\chi_T=-(\partial^{2}\tilde F/\partial\mathcal{H}^{2})_{T,V,N}\geq0$, and the
statistical form is $\Var(M)=k_BT\,\chi_T\geq0$.

\paragraph{Why diamagnets are not counterexamples.} The bound $\chi_T\geq0$ is a
statement about the response of the variable that couples \emph{linearly} to the
field, $\hat H=\hat H_0-\mathcal{H}\hat M$. For an arbitrary
$\mathcal{H}$-dependence, differentiating $\tilde F=-k_BT\ln\Tr\,
e^{-\beta\hat H(\mathcal{H})}$ twice, with $\hat M\equiv-\partial\hat
H/\partial\mathcal{H}$, gives
\[
\chi=\beta\,\Var(\hat M)\;-\;\Big\langle\frac{\partial^{2}\hat H}
{\partial\mathcal{H}^{2}}\Big\rangle .
\]
The first term is the non-negative fluctuation (paramagnetic) response; the
second vanishes only for linear coupling. Orbital magnetism enters through
minimal substitution $\hat{\mathbf{p}}\to\hat{\mathbf{p}}-q\mathbf{A}$, which is
\emph{quadratic} in the field, so $\partial^{2}\hat H/\partial\mathcal{H}^{2}>0$
and the second term is negative --- Landau and core diamagnetism, and in the
extreme case a superconductor. Nothing is violated: what stability of the
combined matter-plus-field system bounds is the permeability
$\mu_r=1+\chi_{\rm vol}\geq0$, with $\chi_{\rm vol}=\chi_T/V$ dimensionless in SI
units, an inequality that $\chi_{\rm vol}=-1$ saturates rather than breaks. (For
$[\hat H_0,\hat M]\neq0$ the variance becomes the Kubo canonical correlation,
likewise non-negative.)

\paragraph{The general pattern.} Applying the construction variable by variable
gives the whole list: $U$ is convex in each of $S,V,N$; $F$ is concave in $T$ and
convex in $V$; $G$ is concave in both $T$ and $P$; $\Omega$ is concave in both
$T$ and $\mu$. Letting two quantities be exchanged at once promotes the
second-order condition to positive semidefiniteness of the Hessian of $U$ in
$(S,V,N)$ --- degenerate along the direction $(S,V,N)$ itself, by Gibbs--Duhem
(Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}) --- from which the cross-inequalities
$C_P\geq C_V\geq0$ and $\kappa_T\geq\kappa_S\geq0$ follow. In every case
\[
\text{curvature of potential} \;=\; \text{response function}
\;=\; \text{variance of the conjugate quantity},
\]
the elementary form of the fluctuation--dissipation theorem. Because the
right-hand side is a variance, statistical mechanics delivers thermodynamic
stability automatically; and equality --- vanishing curvature, diverging
response, diverging fluctuation --- is precisely what marks a critical point.

\section{Statistical ensembles and their partition functions}
\label{sec:ensembles}

Each ensemble corresponds to a choice of which variables the environment fixes,
and computes the corresponding potential as (minus $k_BT$ times) the logarithm of
a partition function.

\paragraph{Microcanonical multiplicity $W$.} For an isolated system,
\[
W(E,V,N)=\#\{\text{microstates with energy in }[E,E+\delta E]\},
\qquad
\boxed{\;S=k_B\ln W\;}
\]

\paragraph{Canonical partition function $Z$.} System exchanging energy with a
bath at temperature $T$, at fixed $N$ and $V$:
\[
Z(T,V,N)=\sum_i e^{-\beta E_i}=\Tr\, e^{-\beta \hat H},
\qquad p_i=\frac{e^{-\beta E_i}}{Z},
\qquad
\boxed{\;F=-k_BT\ln Z\;}
\]

\paragraph{Grand partition function $\Xi$.} System exchanging energy and
particles with a reservoir at $(T,\mu)$, at fixed $V$:
\[
\boxed{\;\Xi(T,V,\mu)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\sum_i e^{-\beta(E_{i,N}-\mu N)}
=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty} z^N Z(T,V,N)\;}
\]
where $z=e^{\beta\mu}$ is the fugacity, with
$p_{i,N}=e^{-\beta(E_{i,N}-\mu N)}/\Xi$ and
\[
\boxed{\;\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi\;}
\]
Consistency check: since $\Omega=-PV$, this says $\ln\Xi=\beta PV$.

\paragraph{Isothermal--isobaric partition function $\Delta$.} System exchanging
energy \emph{and volume} with a reservoir at $(T,P)$, at fixed $N$. Volume is now
a fluctuating variable and the Boltzmann weight acquires the work term $PV$:
\[
\boxed{\;\Delta(T,P,N)=\sum_V e^{-\beta PV}Z(T,V,N)
\;\xrightarrow[\text{continuum}]{}\;
\frac{1}{V_0}\int_0^\infty\! dV\, e^{-\beta PV}Z(T,V,N)\;}
\]
\[
\boxed{\;G=-k_BT\ln\Delta\;}
\]
The reference volume $V_0$ makes the integral dimensionless and contributes a
term of order $\ln V$ to $G$, subextensive and therefore irrelevant in the
thermodynamic limit. Consistency check: $G=\mu N$ gives $\ln\Delta=-\beta\mu N$.
This is the natural ensemble for constant-pressure molecular dynamics.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
Ensemble & Fixed & Fluctuating & Potential \\
\midrule
Microcanonical & $E,V,N$ & --- & $S=k_B\ln W$ \\
Canonical & $T,V,N$ & $E$ & $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ \\
Grand canonical & $T,V,\mu$ & $E,N$ & $\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi$ \\
Isothermal--isobaric & $T,P,N$ & $E,V$ & $G=-k_BT\ln\Delta$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Appendix~\ref{app:bookkeeping} settles three points of bookkeeping that recur:
whether the entropy fluctuates at fixed $T$, why $\Omega$ carries $-\mu N$ when
Euler's relation carries $+\mu N$, and when magnetic work appears as
$\mathcal{H}\,dM$ rather than $-M\,d\mathcal{H}$.

The pattern is a Legendre transform on the thermodynamic side matched by a
Laplace transform on the statistical side: $Z=\sum_E W e^{-\beta E}$,
$\Xi=\sum_N Z e^{\beta\mu N}$, $\Delta=\sum_V Z e^{-\beta PV}$. Evaluating each
Laplace transform by steepest descent in the thermodynamic limit reproduces the
Legendre transform exactly, with the saddle point at the equilibrium value of the
fluctuating variable and the Gaussian width around it giving its variance.

\section{Ensemble equivalence and its limits}
\label{sec:equivalence}

\subsection{Agreement of the potentials}

Take $\Xi=\sum_N e^{-\beta[F(T,V,N)-\mu N]}$ and evaluate by steepest descent.
The exponent is stationary at $N^\ast$ satisfying $(\partial F/\partial
N)_{T,V}=\mu$, which is the thermodynamic condition for particle equilibrium with
the reservoir. Expanding to second order and doing the Gaussian integral,
\[
\Xi\simeq e^{-\beta[F(N^\ast)-\mu N^\ast]}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\beta F''}} ,
\qquad
F''\equiv\left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial N^{2}}\right)_{T,V}
=\left(\frac{\partial\mu}{\partial N}\right)_{T,V},
\]
so
\[
\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi
=\underbrace{F(N^\ast)-\mu N^\ast}_{\text{Legendre transform}}
\;-\;\frac{k_BT}{2}\ln\frac{2\pi}{\beta F''} .
\]
The size of the correction follows from extensivity. At fixed $T$ and $V$, $\mu$
is intensive and $N$ extensive, so $F''=(\partial\mu/\partial N)_{T,V}$ is of
order $1/N$; explicitly $F''=1/\big(\beta\Var(N)\big)$ with $\Var(N)=O(N)$, so
$2\pi/(\beta F'')=2\pi\Var(N)=O(N)$ and the correction is
$-\tfrac12k_BT\ln N+O(1)=\mathcal{O}(\ln N)$. The Gaussian width is $\Delta
N/N\sim N^{-1/2}$, and $\mathcal{O}(\ln N)$ is subextensive, hence invisible in
the densities $\omega=\Omega/V$. That is the whole content of ensemble
equivalence: the two ensembles produce the same thermodynamic surface, up to
subextensive terms.

\subsection{Difference of response functions}
\label{sec:respdiffer}

Here is the point that the $N^{-1/2}$ slogan obscures. Equivalence states that
the \emph{function} $S(T,V,\mu)$ is the same in both descriptions, but a response
function is not determined by the surface alone: it is a \emph{directional
derivative}, fixed by the surface \emph{and} the direction of differentiation.
Fixing $\mu$ and fixing $N$ define two different curves through the same point of
the same surface, so $C_{V,N}=T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,N}$ and
$C_{V,\mu}=T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$ are two different numbers --- exactly
as $C_P\neq C_V$ within a single ensemble, which nobody calls an inequivalence.

The size of the difference is not a $1/N$ correction. From
Section~\ref{sec:caveat},
\[
C_{V,\mu}-C_{V,N}=T\,\frac{\big[(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}\big]^{2}}
{(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}} ,
\]
and both derivatives are extensive: $(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}=O(N)$ and
$(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}=\beta\Var(N)=O(N)$. So the difference is
$O(N^{2}/N)=O(N)$, the same order as the heat capacities themselves. The
resolution is that $N^{-1/2}$ refers to \emph{relative} fluctuations, whereas
response functions are built from \emph{absolute} fluctuations, which are
extensive and do not go away. Saying $\Delta N/N\to0$ is not saying
$\Var(N)\to0$.

\subsection{Inequivalence at a first-order transition}
\label{sec:firstorder}

Here equivalence really does fail, and the mechanism is the Maxwell construction
transplanted to energy space.

At a temperature-driven first-order transition there is a latent heat, so the
ordered and disordered phases have different energy densities $e_o\neq e_d$ at
the same $T_t$. Since they have equal free energy at $T_t$ they carry equal
canonical weight, and the canonical energy distribution
$P(E)\propto W(E)e^{-\beta E}=e^{[S(E)-E/T]/k_B}$ has two peaks, at $E=Ve_o$ and
$E=Ve_d$, separated by a minimum. The dip is the free-energy cost of the
interface between coexisting phases, so the barrier scales as
\[
\Delta F(L)=\ln\frac{P_{\max}}{P_{\min}}\;\sim\;2\sigma L^{d-1},
\]
with $\sigma$ the interfacial tension. This is the Lee--Kosterlitz criterion, the
standard numerical diagnostic for the order of a transition: a barrier that
\emph{grows} with $L$ signals first order, one that stays constant signals a
continuous transition. It is also why multicanonical and Wang--Landau sampling
were invented --- ordinary Metropolis dynamics cannot cross the barrier.

The inequivalence follows at once. Microcanonically, all energies between $Ve_o$
and $Ve_d$ are accessible --- they are phase-separated configurations containing
an interface --- while canonically they are exponentially suppressed and never
typical. In the thermodynamic limit $s(e)$ is \emph{affine} across that interval
(at finite $L$ it has a convex intruder, giving a negative microcanonical
specific heat), so the Legendre transform $s\mapsto f$ is not invertible there:
an entire interval of $e$ maps to the single temperature $T_t$. The canonical
ensemble cannot resolve which $e$; the microcanonical can, so it is strictly
finer and information is genuinely lost. The affine segment of $s(e)$ is the
exact analogue of the flat portion of the convex hull of $F(V)$ --- the same
theorem, not an analogy.

Appendix~\ref{app:histograms} derives, from a single principle, how many peaks
$P(E)$ and $P(\phi)$ have in each situation --- two questions that behave
oppositely and are routinely conflated.

At a continuous transition the ensembles agree on the free energy but can
disagree on the critical \emph{exponents}: a hidden constraint such as fixed
composition renormalises them, by $\alpha_c=-\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ and
$\nu_c=\nu/(1-\alpha)$, whenever $\alpha>0$. This is Fisher renormalisation, the
critical-point face of the $c_{V,\mu}$ versus $c_{V,N}$ distinction; it is
derived in Appendix~\ref{app:fisher}. Equivalence is a statement about
thermodynamic potentials, never a licence to interchange derivatives.

\begin{remark}
Equivalence also fails, for a different reason, for systems with long-range
interactions (self-gravitating matter, unscreened plasmas, infinite-range spin
models). There $s(e)$ can be genuinely non-concave even in the thermodynamic
limit, so the microcanonical ensemble supports stable states with negative
specific heat that have no canonical counterpart at all.
\end{remark}

\section{Finite-size scaling}
\label{sec:fss}

\subsection{Absence of a transition in a finite system}
\label{sec:fss:problem}

For any finite system $Z$ is a finite sum of positive exponentials, hence entire
in $\beta$ with no zeros on the real axis, so $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is analytic in $T$
and every derivative of it is finite. The consequences are not small print:
$c(T,L)$ and $\chi(T,L)$ have finite maxima at every $L$;
$\langle|m|\rangle(T,L)>0$ at every temperature, so there is no sharp point below
which order switches on; and there is strictly no $T_c$ --- the maximum of $c$,
the maximum of $\chi$ and the inflection of $\langle|m|\rangle$ are three
different temperatures. So the naive programme, simulating near $T_c$ and fitting
$c\sim|t|^{-\alpha}$, fits a power law to a function that has none; what it
measures is the \emph{rounding} of the singularity, an artifact whose magnitude
depends on $L$, and no amount of statistics repairs the resulting drift.

Finite-size scaling turns the obstacle into the instrument. The rounding is
controlled by one mechanism, it is universal, and its $L$-dependence contains
exactly the exponents wanted. And $L$ is an integer the simulator chooses
exactly, whereas $t=(T-T_c)/T_c$ requires prior knowledge of $T_c$ --- which is
what is not known. Most of the accuracy of modern exponent determinations comes
from this exchange of an unknown continuous variable for a known discrete one.

\subsection{The rounding-and-shifting estimate}
\label{sec:fss:estimate}

In the infinite system $\xi(t)\simeq\xi_0|t|^{-\nu}$; in a box of side $L$
correlations cannot extend further than the system. So there are two regimes,
separated by $\xi(t)\approx L$, i.e.\ by
\[
\boxed{\;|t^{\ast}(L)|\;\approx\;\left(\frac{\xi_0}{L}\right)^{1/\nu}
\;\sim\;L^{-1/\nu}\;}
\]
For $|t|\gg|t^{\ast}|$ the correlated regions fit inside the box, the system
cannot tell it is finite, and bulk power laws are observed: a finite sample does
reproduce thermodynamic-limit behaviour, just not arbitrarily close to $T_c$. For
$|t|\lesssim|t^{\ast}|$, $L$ takes over as the only length and every singular
quantity saturates at roughly the value it had when $\xi\approx L$. Saturation
converts a divergence in $t$ into a power of $L$ by substitution,
$\chi\sim|t^{\ast}|^{-\gamma}\sim L^{\gamma/\nu}$, and identically
$c_{\max}\sim L^{\alpha/\nu}$ and
$\langle|m|\rangle\big|_{T_c}\sim L^{-\beta/\nu}$; the rounded peak has width
$\Delta T\sim L^{-1/\nu}$ in temperature and is displaced from $T_c$ by the same
order, there being no other scale to set it.

The estimate delivers the exponents of $L$ and nothing else: no amplitudes, no
peak shape, no size of corrections --- and it is the corrections, not the leading
powers, that set the achievable accuracy.

\subsection{The five assumptions}
\label{sec:fss:assumptions}

Finite-size scaling rests on five assumptions, set out in full in
Appendix~\ref{app:fssassumptions}. In brief:
\begin{itemize}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
\item[(S1)] a \emph{single} divergent length, $\xi(t)\simeq\xi_0|t|^{-\nu}$, with
the order-parameter correlator $G(r)\simeq A\,r^{-(d-2+\eta)}g(r/\xi)$ and every
other length --- lattice spacing, interaction range, interface width --- staying
finite as $t\to0$;
\item[(S2)] one $k_BT$ of singular free energy per correlation volume,
$f_{\rm sing}\approx k_BT\,\xi^{-d}$, which together with (S1) is \emph{exactly}
hyperscaling,
\[
f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{d\nu}\ \ \text{and}\ \ f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{2-\alpha}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;2-\alpha=d\nu\;}
\]
\item[(S3)] $L$ enters only through the ratio $L/\xi$. Half of this is exact:
coarse-graining by $b$ turns a box of $L^{d}$ sites into one of $(L/b)^{d}$, so
$L^{-1}$ has scaling eigenvalue exactly $y_L=1$, with no anomalous dimension,
because $L$ is a geometric parameter and not a coupling that gets renormalised.
The assumption is that no \emph{new} singular $L$-dependence appears beyond it;
\item[(S4)] fixed geometry, aspect ratio and boundary conditions --- the scaling
function below is universal only \emph{given} these, and periodic boundaries are
standard because they remove the surface term entirely;
\item[(S5)] corrections come from irrelevant scaling fields, contributing
relative $L^{-\omega}$ ($\omega\simeq0.83$ for three-dimensional Ising, hence
still $6\%$ at $L=32$), and from a smooth analytic background that matters for
$c$ and not for $\chi$.
\end{itemize}
Each fails somewhere, and Appendix~\ref{app:fsspractice} collects those places.

\subsection{The fundamental scaling relation}
\label{sec:fss:fundamental}

Assemble (S1)--(S3). By (S2) the singular free energy density is $\xi^{-d}$ times
a dimensionless function; by (S3) the only dimensionless variable $L$ can supply
is $L/\xi$; and it is convenient to pull out $L^{-d}$ rather than $\xi^{-d}$, the
two differing by a power of $L/\xi$ absorbed into the function. Including the
field, with scaling eigenvalue $y_h$,
\[
\boxed{\;
f_{\rm sing}(t,h,L)\;=\;L^{-d}\,\mathfrak{F}\!\left(t\,L^{1/\nu},\;h\,L^{y_h}\right)
\;}
\tag{FSS}
\]
with $\mathfrak{F}$ a universal function fixed by dimensionality, symmetry,
geometry and boundary conditions and by nothing else. This one equation is the
method.

\paragraph{The bulk limit and hyperscaling.} Fix $t\neq0$ and let $L\to\infty$;
$f_{\rm sing}$ must become $L$-independent. Writing $x=tL^{1/\nu}$, so
$L^{-d}=(t/x)^{d\nu}$,
\[
f_{\rm sing}=t^{d\nu}\Big[x^{-d\nu}\,\mathfrak{F}\big(x,\;h\,(x/t)^{\nu y_h}\big)\Big],
\]
and $L$-independence requires
$\mathfrak{F}(x,y)\to|x|^{d\nu}\Psi\big(y/|x|^{\nu y_h}\big)$ at large $|x|$,
whence
\[
f_{\rm sing}(t,h)=|t|^{d\nu}\,\Psi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\nu y_h}}\right)
\;\equiv\;|t|^{2-\alpha}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\Delta}}\right),
\]
recovering the homogeneous form of Section~\ref{sec:critical} and identifying
$2-\alpha=d\nu$ and $\Delta=\nu y_h$. So the $L^{-d}$ prefactor in (FSS)
\emph{is} hyperscaling: assuming one is assuming the other, and wherever
hyperscaling fails --- above the upper critical dimension, most importantly ---
(FSS) must be modified.

Two exponents suffice. Taking $\nu$ and $\eta$ as primary --- they are the pair
measurable with the highest accuracy --- the scaling and hyperscaling relations
give
\[
\boxed{\;\frac{\gamma}{\nu}=2-\eta,\qquad
\frac{\beta}{\nu}=\frac{d-2+\eta}{2},\qquad
y_h=\frac{d+2-\eta}{2},\qquad
y_t=\frac{1}{\nu},\qquad
\frac{\alpha}{\nu}=\frac{2}{\nu}-d\;}
\]
the first of these being the Fisher relation; Appendix~\ref{app:fsspractice}
derives them. Every exponent appearing below is one of these combinations, so
there are no free parameters beyond $\nu$ and $\eta$ --- plus the correction
exponent $\omega$ and the non-universal amplitudes.

\subsection{The observables}
\label{sec:fss:observables}

Everything follows by differentiating (FSS). Throughout $x\equiv tL^{1/\nu}$, all
relations hold at $h=0$ unless stated, and script letters denote universal
functions of $x$, each carrying one non-universal amplitude that is not
predicted.

\paragraph{Order parameter.} Differentiating with respect to $h$ at $h=0$ gives
$m=L^{\,y_h-d}\,\mathfrak{M}(x)=L^{-\beta/\nu}\,\mathfrak{M}(x)$, since
$y_h-d=-(d-2+\eta)/2=-\beta/\nu$. One caution is essential: in a finite system
with exact $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry and $h=0$, $\langle m\rangle=0$ identically,
the two ordered states being sampled with equal weight. The measurable surrogates
are $\langle|m|\rangle$ and $\langle m^{2}\rangle^{1/2}$; both scale as
$L^{-\beta/\nu}$ with the same exponent but different amplitudes and different
corrections, and must not be interchanged within one analysis.

\paragraph{Susceptibility.} Differentiating twice,
$\chi=L^{2y_h-d}\mathfrak{X}(x)=L^{\gamma/\nu}\mathfrak{X}(x)$ with
$2y_h-d=2-\eta=\gamma/\nu$. At $h=0$ with exact symmetry the correct fluctuation
formula is
$\chi=\beta V\langle m^{2}\rangle$, since $\langle m\rangle=0$ makes
$\Var(m)=\langle m^{2}\rangle$; the variant
$\chi'=\beta V(\langle m^{2}\rangle-\langle|m|\rangle^{2})$ in circulation is a
different finite-size quantity with the same exponent but a different amplitude.

\paragraph{Specific heat.} Differentiating twice with respect to $t$ and
restoring the background of (S5),
\[
c=c_{\rm reg}(T)+L^{\alpha/\nu}\,\mathfrak{C}(x) .
\]
If $\alpha>0$ the singular term grows and eventually dominates. If $\alpha=0$ the
power law degenerates and the correct form is $c=c_{\rm reg}+A\ln L+\dots$ (the
two-dimensional Ising case). If $\alpha<0$ the singular term \emph{shrinks} and
the constant dominates at every accessible $L$, so a log--log fit of $c_{\max}$
against $L$ measures the background, not the exponent.

\paragraph{Correlation length and dimensionless ratios.} $\xi$ is itself a
length, so $\xi_L/L=\mathfrak{Z}(x)$: a dimensionless quantity with \emph{no}
power of $L$ in front. Likewise, since $\langle
m^{k}\rangle=L^{-k\beta/\nu}\mathfrak{M}_k(x)$, any ratio in which the powers of
$L$ cancel is a pure function of $x$. The canonical example is the fourth-order
Binder cumulant
\[
U_4\;\equiv\;1-\frac{\langle m^{4}\rangle}{3\langle m^{2}\rangle^{2}}
=\mathfrak{U}(x) .
\]
Three consequences form the backbone of the method. First, $\mathfrak{U}$ is
universal outright, not up to a non-universal factor, so nothing must be fitted
away. Second, at $t=0$ one has $x=0$ for every $L$, so
$U_4(T_c,L)=\mathfrak{U}(0)\equiv U_4^{\ast}$ independently of $L$: plotting
$U_4$ against $T$ for several $L$ gives a common crossing that locates $T_c$
\emph{without} prior knowledge of it, and delivers $U_4^{\ast}$ as a
universality-class fingerprint (Figure~\ref{fig:fss}c). This is the single most
useful consequence of (FSS). Third, $\partial U_4/\partial
T=L^{1/\nu}\mathfrak{U}'(x)/T_c$, so $[\partial U_4/\partial T]_{\max}\sim
L^{1/\nu}$ gives $\nu$ from a pure power of $L$. The same logic applies to
$\xi_L/L$ and to any RG-invariant ratio, and checking that two or three of them
agree is the standard test that corrections are under control.

\paragraph{Peak scaling without $T_c$.} Consider $A(t,L)=L^{\rho}\mathfrak{a}(x)$
whose scaling function has a maximum at $x=x_{\max}$, a pure number fixed by
$\mathfrak{a}$ and hence universal. Then
\[
\boxed{\;
A_{\max}(L)=\mathfrak{a}(x_{\max})\,L^{\rho},
\qquad
T_{\max}(L)=T_c\Big(1+x_{\max}L^{-1/\nu}\Big)
\;}
\]
The \emph{height} of the peak is a pure power of $L$, requiring no knowledge of
$T_c$ whatsoever: one locates the maximum, an operational procedure, and plots
its height against $L$. The \emph{position} approaches $T_c$ as $L^{-1/\nu}$, so
a two-parameter fit of $T_{\max}(L)$ yields $T_c$ and $\nu$ simultaneously. Thus
$\chi_{\max}\sim L^{\gamma/\nu}$ and $[\partial U_4/\partial T]_{\max}\sim
L^{1/\nu}$ are exponent measurements insensitive to where $T_c$ is. Note that
$x_{\max}$ differs from observable to observable: the maxima of $\chi$, of $c$
and of $\partial U_4/\partial T$ occur at three \emph{different} pseudo-critical
temperatures, converging to the same $T_c$ at rate $L^{-1/\nu}$ but from
different directions. Treating them as one temperature is a frequent error.

measurements; the height needs no knowledge of $T_c$. \textbf{(b)} The same data
plotted as $L^{-\gamma/\nu}\chi$ against $x=tL^{1/\nu}$ collapse onto the single
universal function $\mathfrak{X}$ --- an excellent diagnostic, a poor estimator.

its scaling function: $U_4=\mathfrak{U}(tL^{1/\nu})$. Hence all curves pass
through the same point at $t=0$, locating $T_c$ and the universal value
$U_4^{\ast}$ without any fit, while the slope at the crossing grows as

from a real universality class.}
