The rest of the method is collected in Appendix~\ref{app:fsspractice}: the
scaling relations quoted above; two further estimators (logarithmic derivatives,
which extract $1/\nu$ with no peak-location step, and data collapse, which is a
diagnostic rather than a fit, illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:fss}b); the
corrections to scaling that set the achievable accuracy; the modified scaling
that replaces (FSS) at a first-order transition, with the table of discriminants
used to tell the two apart; the boundaries beyond which (FSS) does not hold at
all; and the standard protocol for extracting exponents numerically.

\section{Heat capacity at fixed \texorpdfstring{$N$}{N} and at fixed \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu}}

We use the volumetric (per unit volume) heat capacity
\[
\boxed{\;c_V=\frac{1}{V}\left(\frac{\dbar Q}{dT}\right)_V
=\frac{T}{V}\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}\right)_V\;}
\]
The second form is preferable as a definition: it refers only to state functions
and carries no hidden reversibility assumption. The subscript $V$ is not the whole
story --- one must also say what else is held fixed.

\subsection{Canonical ensemble: fixed \texorpdfstring{$N$}{N}}
\label{sec:canonical}

With $N$ fixed and $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$, inserting $F=-k_BT\ln Z$
and changing variables to $\beta$ with $d\beta/dT=-k_B\beta^{2}$,
\[
\boxed{\;c_V=-\frac{T}{V}\left(\frac{\partial^2F}{\partial T^2}\right)_{V,N}
=\frac{k_B\beta^{2}}{V}
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\ln Z}{\partial\beta^{2}}\right)_{V,N}\;}
\]
Since $\partial\ln Z/\partial\beta=-\langle E\rangle$ and
$\partial^{2}\ln Z/\partial\beta^{2}=\Var(E)$,
\[
\boxed{\;c_V=\frac{\Var(E)}{Vk_BT^{2}}\;}
\]
The heat capacity is the energy variance: a system that absorbs a lot of heat per
kelvin is one whose energy fluctuates a lot at fixed temperature. Positivity is
automatic.

\subsection{Grand canonical ensemble: fixed \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu}}

Here $N$ fluctuates while $T,V,\mu$ are fixed. At constant volume
$dU=T\,dS+\mu\,dN$, so $d(U-\mu N)=T\,dS$ at fixed $\mu$ and the heat absorbed is
$\dbar Q_{\rm rev}=dU-\mu\,dN$: the energy change minus the part carried in by
the incoming particles. Hence, with $S=-(\partial\Omega/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$,
\[
c_V=\frac{1}{V}\left(\frac{\partial (U-\mu N)}{\partial T}\right)_{V,\mu}
=\frac{T}{V}\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}\right)_{V,\mu}
=-\frac{T}{V}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\Omega}{\partial T^{2}}\right)_{V,\mu} .
\]

Splitting the energy flux of an open system into ``heat'' and ``matter
transport'' is a matter of convention, because particles entering carry entropy
as well as energy; the expression above is the definition $\dbar Q\equiv T\,dS$
at fixed $\mu$, which is self-consistent and standard, while other conventions
(using the enthalpy per particle rather than $\mu$) appear in the
thermoelectric literature.

With $\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi$, exactly as in the canonical case,
$c_V=(k_B\beta^{2}/V)(\partial^{2}\ln\Xi/\partial\beta^{2})_{V,\mu}$;
differentiating at fixed $\mu$ --- \emph{not} at fixed $\beta\mu$, a frequent
slip --- gives $\partial^{2}\ln\Xi/\partial\beta^{2}|_{\mu}=\Var(E-\mu N)$, so
\[
\boxed{\;c_{V,\mu}=\frac{\Var(E-\mu N)}{Vk_BT^{2}}
=\frac{\Var(E)+\mu^{2}\Var(N)-2\mu\,\Cov(E,N)}{Vk_BT^{2}}\;}
\]
The isothermal--isobaric ensemble completes the pattern, $\Var(E+PV)/Vk_BT^{2}$
giving $c_P$.

\subsection{Inequality of \texorpdfstring{$c_{V,\mu}$}{cVmu} and \texorpdfstring{$c_{V,N}$}{cVN}}
\label{sec:caveat}

The two boxed results are not two computations of the same quantity;
Section~\ref{sec:respdiffer} explains why this is consistent with ensemble
equivalence. Writing $S$ and $N$ as functions of $(T,V,\mu)$ and using the
Maxwell relation $(\partial S/\partial\mu)_{T,V}=(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$,
\[
\boxed{\;C_{V,N}=C_{V,\mu}
-\;T\,\frac{\big[(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}\big]^{2}}
{(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}}\;}
\]
Because $(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}=\beta\Var(N)>0$, the correction is
strictly negative: $C_{V,N}\leq C_{V,\mu}$, as stability requires. In fluctuation
language, using $(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}=k_B\beta^{2}\Cov(N,E-\mu N)$,
the $\mu$-dependence cancels identically and
\[
\boxed{\;c_{V,N}=\frac{1}{Vk_BT^{2}}
\left[\Var(E)-\frac{\Cov(E,N)^{2}}{\Var(N)}\right]\;}
\]
--- the energy variance with the part explained by the particle-number
fluctuation projected out, i.e.\ a conditional variance.

Appendix~\ref{app:bookkeeping} checks both formulas against the classical ideal
gas, where the naive grand-canonical answer is not even $\mu$-independent and the
correction term restores $\tfrac32Nk_B$ exactly.

\section{Microscopic view: thermodynamics as a filter on the spectrum}
\label{sec:filters}

Everything so far has been top-down: a potential is postulated, Legendre
transformed, and differentiated. This section runs the chain in the opposite
direction for the one case in which it can be run explicitly --- a system of
independent fermionic levels in the grand canonical ensemble --- and asks what a
measurement at temperature $T$ actually \emph{sees} of the spectrum. The answer
is sharper than the usual slogan that thermodynamics probes states within $k_BT$
of the chemical potential. Each observable turns out to be the spectrum viewed
through its \emph{own} kernel: the kernels are all centred on $\mu$ and all have a
width set by $T$ alone and by no property of the spectrum, but they differ in
shape, and the differences are physical. The heat capacity, in particular, is
exactly blind to the levels sitting at $\mu$.

Two remarks on scope before the derivation. The per-level factorisation below is
special to a Hamiltonian quadratic in the fermion operators; the fluctuation
identity it produces, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C_fluct}, is completely general and holds
for any grand canonical system. And the whole construction is at fixed $\mu$;
Section~\ref{sec:filt_muN} converts to fixed $N$ using the formula of
Section~\ref{sec:caveat}, which is not always a small correction.

\subsection{The two-level decomposition}
\label{sec:filt_twolevel}

Take
\[
\hat H=\sum_\lambda\varepsilon_\lambda\,\hat n_\lambda ,
\qquad
\hat N=\sum_\lambda\hat n_\lambda ,
\qquad
\hat n_\lambda=c^\dagger_\lambda c^{\phantom\dagger}_\lambda ,
\]
where $\lambda$ runs over single-particle orbitals (spin and any other internal
label included) and $\hat n_\lambda$ has eigenvalues $0,1$. Nothing is idealised
in the step: for any quadratic Hamiltonian the $\varepsilon_\lambda$ are its
exact eigenvalues, including in a disordered or inhomogeneous sample where they
are not labelled by momentum.

Because $\hat H-\mu\hat N=\sum_\lambda(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)\hat n_\lambda$ is
a sum of commuting one-orbital terms, the trace defining $\Xi$ factorises over
$\lambda$ and each factor is a two-state sum, $\hat n_\lambda\in\{0,1\}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_Xi}
\Xi(T,\mu)=\prod_\lambda\Big(1+e^{-\beta(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)}\Big),
\qquad
\Omega=-k_BT\sum_\lambda\ln\Big(1+e^{-\beta(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)}\Big).
\end{equation}
The orbitals are statistically independent --- a fact used twice below, once for
the entropy and once for the variance.

Introduce the dimensionless distance from the chemical potential,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_x}
\boxed{\;x_\lambda\equiv\frac{\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu}{2k_BT}\;}
\end{equation}
so that $\beta(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)=2x_\lambda$. The factor of two is not
cosmetic. A single orbital in the grand canonical ensemble is a two-level system
with levels $0$ and $\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu$, i.e.\ a spin-$\tfrac12$ in a field,
and $x_\lambda$ is the distance from the \emph{midpoint} of that pair, the
variable in which the problem is manifestly symmetric. Extracting the midpoint is
exactly the algebraic identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_identity}
\ln\big(1+e^{-2x}\big)
=\ln\Big(e^{-x}\big(e^{x}+e^{-x}\big)\Big)
=-x+\ln\big(2\cosh x\big),
\end{equation}
whose first term is the $T$-independent zero-point piece and whose second is the
entire thermal content. Substituting into Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_Xi},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_Omega}
\Omega(T,\mu)=\sum_\lambda\Big[\;\tfrac12\big(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu\big)
\;-\;k_BT\,\ln\big(2\cosh x_\lambda\big)\;\Big].
\end{equation}
Each orbital contributes its own two-level grand potential, and no expansion or
approximation has been made.

\paragraph{Entropy.} Differentiate at fixed $\mu$. Since $x_\lambda\propto1/T$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_dxdT}
\left(\frac{\partial x_\lambda}{\partial T}\right)_{\!\mu}=-\frac{x_\lambda}{T},
\end{equation}
which is the one relation the rest of the subsection rests on. The first term of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_Omega} is temperature independent, so with
$S=-(\partial\Omega/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$,
\[
-\frac{\partial}{\partial T}\Big[-k_BT\ln(2\cosh x_\lambda)\Big]_\mu
=k_B\ln(2\cosh x_\lambda)+k_BT\tanh x_\lambda
\left(\frac{\partial x_\lambda}{\partial T}\right)_{\!\mu},
\]
and inserting Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_dxdT},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_S}
S(T,\mu)=k_B\sum_\lambda\Big[\;\ln\big(2\cosh x_\lambda\big)
\;-\;x_\lambda\tanh x_\lambda\;\Big].
\end{equation}
It is worth checking this against the Gibbs entropy of independent orbitals,
$S=-k_B\sum_\lambda[\,n_\lambda\ln n_\lambda+(1-n_\lambda)\ln(1-n_\lambda)\,]$.
The Fermi factor written in the symmetric variable is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_n}
n_\lambda=\frac{1}{1+e^{2x_\lambda}}=\frac{e^{-x_\lambda}}{2\cosh x_\lambda},
\qquad
1-n_\lambda=\frac{e^{x_\lambda}}{2\cosh x_\lambda},
\qquad
1-2n_\lambda=\tanh x_\lambda ,
\end{equation}
so $\ln n_\lambda=-x_\lambda-\ln(2\cosh x_\lambda)$ and
$\ln(1-n_\lambda)=+x_\lambda-\ln(2\cosh x_\lambda)$, whence the bracket is
$\ln(2\cosh x_\lambda)-x_\lambda(1-2n_\lambda)$, which is
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_S}. The two routes agree, as they must.

\paragraph{Heat capacity.} Write the per-orbital entropy as
$s(x)=k_B[\ln(2\cosh x)-x\tanh x]$. Its derivative telescopes,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_sprime}
\frac{ds}{dx}=k_B\big[\tanh x-\tanh x-x\operatorname{sech}^2x\big]
=-k_B\,x\operatorname{sech}^2x ,
\end{equation}
the cancellation being the reason the entropy filter is smooth at its maximum.
With $C_\mu\equiv T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$ and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_dxdT},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_C}
\boxed{\;C_\mu(T)=k_B\sum_\lambda x_\lambda^{2}\operatorname{sech}^2x_\lambda\;}
\end{equation}
Every term is non-negative, so $C_\mu>0$ level by level --- the stability result
of Section~\ref{sec:convexity}, here without any thermodynamic argument.

\paragraph{The same quantity as a fluctuation.} Section~\ref{sec:caveat} gives
$C_\mu=\Var(E-\mu N)/k_BT^{2}$ for any grand canonical system. For independent
orbitals $\hat H-\mu\hat N=\sum_\lambda(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)\hat n_\lambda$
with independent $\hat n_\lambda$, and since $\hat n_\lambda^2=\hat n_\lambda$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_var_n}
\Var(\hat n_\lambda)=n_\lambda(1-n_\lambda)
=\frac{e^{-x_\lambda}e^{x_\lambda}}{4\cosh^2x_\lambda}
=\tfrac14\operatorname{sech}^2x_\lambda
=-k_BT\,\frac{\partial n_\lambda}{\partial\varepsilon_\lambda}\,,
\end{equation}
so that, using $(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)^2=4(k_BT)^2x_\lambda^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_C_fluct}
\frac{\Var(E-\mu N)}{k_BT^{2}}
=\frac{1}{k_BT^{2}}\sum_\lambda(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)^2\,
\tfrac14\operatorname{sech}^2x_\lambda
=k_B\sum_\lambda x_\lambda^{2}\operatorname{sech}^2x_\lambda ,
\end{equation}
identical to Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C}. The two readings of the same number ---
a sum of two-level Schottky contributions, and a variance of $E-\mu N$ --- are
the two halves of the discussion in Section~\ref{sec:filt_blind}.

\subsection{The filters}
\label{sec:filt_kernels}

Introduce the single-particle density of states
$\rho(\varepsilon)=\sum_\lambda\delta(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_\lambda)$ and
replace the sums by integrals. Equations~\eqref{eq:filt_S} and~\eqref{eq:filt_C},
together with $N=\sum_\lambda n_\lambda$, become
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_integrals}
\boxed{\;
\mathcal{O}(T,\mu)=\int\!d\varepsilon\;\rho(\varepsilon)\;
w_{\mathcal{O}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon-\mu}{2k_BT}\right)\;}
\end{equation}
with the four kernels
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_kernels}
\begin{aligned}
C_\mu:&\ \ w_c(x)=k_B\,x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x, &
S:&\ \ w_S(x)=k_B\big[\ln(2\cosh x)-x\tanh x\big],\\[2pt]
\left(\tfrac{\partial N}{\partial T}\right)_{\!\mu}\!:&\ \
w_{N,T}(x)=\frac{x\operatorname{sech}^{2}x}{2T}, &
\left(\tfrac{\partial N}{\partial\mu}\right)_{\!T}\!:&\ \
w_{N,\mu}(x)=\frac{\operatorname{sech}^{2}x}{4k_BT} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The last two follow from Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_n} by the same one-line
differentiations, $\partial n/\partial x=-\tfrac12\operatorname{sech}^2x$
together with Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_dxdT} and
$(\partial x/\partial\mu)_T=-1/2k_BT$; they are needed in
Section~\ref{sec:filt_muN}.

Three structural facts hold for all four.

\paragraph{(i) The width is set by $T$ alone.} Every kernel is a fixed function of
$(\varepsilon-\mu)/2k_BT$. The spectrum enters Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} only
through $\rho$; it contributes nothing to the resolution of the instrument. This
is the scale covariance of the Boltzmann weight, seen one level at a time: a
change of temperature dilates the kernel about $\mu$ without deforming it.

\paragraph{(ii) Parity is fixed by the observable.} $w_c$, $w_S$ and $w_{N,\mu}$
are even; $w_{N,T}$ is odd. Consequently the even observables are sensitive to
$\rho$ symmetrised about $\mu$, and $(\partial N/\partial T)_\mu$ measures the
\emph{asymmetry} of $\rho$ across the window --- it vanishes identically for a
particle--hole symmetric spectrum, which is why the fixed-$N$ correction of
Section~\ref{sec:caveat} disappears at half filling.

\paragraph{(iii) The shapes differ, and the difference is physical.} Collecting
the relevant constants:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
Kernel & value at $\varepsilon=\mu$ & peak at $|\varepsilon-\mu|$
& peak height & $\int\!d\varepsilon\,w$ \\
\midrule
$w_c/k_B$ & $0$ & $2.3994\,k_BT$ & $0.43923$ & $\pi^{2}k_BT/3$ \\
$w_S/k_B$ & $\ln2$ & $0$ & $\ln 2$ & $\pi^{2}k_BT/3$ \\
$w_{N,\mu}$ & $1/4k_BT$ & $0$ & $1/4k_BT$ & $1$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each entry is elementary.
$w_c'\propto 2x\operatorname{sech}^2x\,(1-x\tanh x)$ vanishes at the root $x_\ast$
of $x\tanh x=1$, namely $x_\ast=1.199679$, i.e.\
$|\varepsilon-\mu|=2x_\ast k_BT=2.3994\,k_BT$; at that point
$\operatorname{sech}^2x_\ast=1-\tanh^2x_\ast=1-x_\ast^{-2}$, so the height is
exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_peak}
\max_x\,\frac{w_c}{k_B}=x_\ast^{2}-1=0.439229\ldots
\end{equation}
The areas follow from $d\varepsilon=2k_BT\,dx$ and
$\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x\,dx=\pi^{2}/6$, while
$\int w_S\,dx$ reduces to the same integral by parts, using
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sprime}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_byparts}
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{w_S}{k_B}\,dx
=\Big[x\,\frac{w_S}{k_B}\Big]_{-\infty}^{\infty}
+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\! x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x\,dx
=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6},
\end{equation}
the boundary term vanishing because $w_S\simeq 2k_B|x|e^{-2|x|}$ at large $|x|$.
Finally $\int\operatorname{sech}^2x\,dx=2$ gives unit area for $w_{N,\mu}$.

Three consequences deserve to be stated separately.

The unit area of $w_{N,\mu}$ is the statement
$(\partial N/\partial\mu)_T\to\rho(\mu)$ for a smooth $\rho$: the compressibility
counts states at the Fermi level with weight one, and it is the only one of the
four that does.

The equal areas of $w_c$ and $w_S$ are the reason that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_sommerfeld}
C_\mu\;\simeq\;S\;\simeq\;\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}k_B^{2}T\,\rho(\mu)
\qquad(\rho\text{ smooth on the scale }k_BT),
\end{equation}
which is the Sommerfeld result, here obtained without a Sommerfeld expansion: if
$\rho$ is constant across the window it comes out of the integral and only the
kernel area survives. That $S$ and $C_\mu$ coincide at this order, despite
weighting entirely different levels, is a coincidence of areas and nothing
deeper; Section~\ref{sec:filt_power} shows it fail as soon as $\rho$ has
structure.

The area of $w_c$ is $\pi^{2}k_BT/3\approx3.29\,k_BT$, which is the precise
version of ``the specific heat counts the levels within $k_BT$ of the chemical
potential'': the exact statement is that $C_\mu/k_B$ equals the number of levels
in a \emph{box} of width $3.29\,k_BT$ centred on $\mu$ and of unit weight. The
actual kernel is not that box, and the difference is the subject of the next
subsection.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,x=1.30cm,y=4.30cm]
  \draw[->] (-4.3,0) -- (4.5,0) node[below right]{$x=(\varepsilon-\mu)/2k_BT$};
  \draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,0.83) node[left]{$w/k_B$};
  \foreach \t in {-4,-3,-2,-1,1,2,3,4}
    \draw (\t,0) -- (\t,-0.012) node[below,font=\scriptsize]{$\t$};
  \draw[dotted,gray] (-4.3,0.6931) -- (4.3,0.6931);
  \node[anchor=east,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1.5pt] at (-4.32,0.6931) {$\ln2$};
  \draw[dotted,gray] (-4.3,0.4392) -- (4.3,0.4392);
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1.5pt] at (4.32,0.4392) {$0.4392$};
  \draw[very thick,blue!60!black,domain=-4.3:4.3,samples=180,smooth,variable=\x]
       plot ({\x},{ln(2*cosh(\x))-\x*tanh(\x)});
  \draw[very thick,red!75!black,domain=-4.3:4.3,samples=180,smooth,variable=\x]
       plot ({\x},{\x*\x/(cosh(\x)*cosh(\x))});
  \draw[dotted,gray!80] (1.1997,0) -- (1.1997,0.4392);
  \draw[dotted,gray!80] (-1.1997,0) -- (-1.1997,0.4392);
  \fill[red!75!black] (1.1997,0.4392) circle (1.4pt);
  \fill[red!75!black] (-1.1997,0.4392) circle (1.4pt);
  \fill[blue!60!black] (0,0.6931) circle (1.4pt);
  \node[anchor=south west,font=\scriptsize,blue!60!black,inner sep=2pt]
       at (1.15,0.74) {$w_S$: entropy};
  \draw[blue!60!black,thin] (1.13,0.76) -- (0.55,0.65);
  \node[anchor=south west,font=\scriptsize,red!75!black,inner sep=2pt]
       at (2.10,0.30) {$w_c$: heat capacity};
  \draw[red!75!black,thin] (2.08,0.33) -- (1.45,0.42);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The two kernels of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_kernels}, in units of $k_B$,
against the symmetric variable $x=(\varepsilon-\mu)/2k_BT$. Both are even, both
are centred on $\mu$, both have width set by $T$ alone, and both enclose the same
area $\pi^{2}/6$. The entropy kernel peaks at $\mu$ with height $\ln2$ --- one
undecided orbital carries one bit. The heat-capacity kernel \emph{vanishes} at
$\mu$ and peaks at $|\varepsilon-\mu|=2.3994\,k_BT$ with height $x_\ast^2-1$.}
\label{fig:filt_kernels}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Why the heat capacity is blind to the levels at \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu}}
\label{sec:filt_blind}

The vanishing of $w_c$ at $\varepsilon=\mu$ --- visible in
Fig.~\ref{fig:filt_kernels}, and easy to miss because it contradicts the usual
picture of $c_V$ as a count of thermally ``available'' states --- is explained by
the fluctuation form, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C_fluct}. Each orbital contributes a
product of two factors,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_product}
\underbrace{(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)^{2}}_{\text{energy cost}}
\;\times\;
\underbrace{n_\lambda(1-n_\lambda)}_{\text{occupancy variance}}\,,
\end{equation}
and the two are maximised in different places. The occupancy variance is
$\tfrac14\operatorname{sech}^2x_\lambda$: it is largest for a level exactly at
$\mu$, which is a fair coin, and exponentially small for a level far below $\mu$
(occupied with certainty) or far above it (empty with certainty). Neither of the
latter fluctuates, so neither can absorb heat. But a level exactly at $\mu$,
while maximally undecided, costs nothing to occupy: flipping it moves no energy
in the grand canonical sense, and it too absorbs no heat.

\begin{quote}
A heat measurement sees only the levels that are simultaneously
\emph{undecided} and \emph{displaced}; $|\varepsilon-\mu|=2.4\,k_BT$ is where
that compromise is optimal.
\end{quote}

Availability alone is therefore not sufficient. The observable that counts
undecided levels without the energy weighting is the entropy, whose kernel is
peaked at $\mu$ with the value $\ln2$ --- one orbital with $n=\tfrac12$ carries
exactly one bit. Two observables, two questions: $S$ asks how many levels are
undecided, $C_\mu$ asks how much energy their indecision can move.

The same structure explains the exponential decay on both sides. The rate is set
by the occupancy variance, $\operatorname{sech}^2x\simeq4e^{-2|x|}$, i.e.\
$e^{-|\varepsilon-\mu|/k_BT}$ in physical units: a level a few $k_BT$ from the
chemical potential is frozen, and the polynomial prefactors ($x^2$ for $w_c$,
$2|x|$ for $w_S$) only decide how the two kernels approach that common fate.

\subsection{Fixed \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu} versus fixed \texorpdfstring{$N$}{N}}
\label{sec:filt_muN}

Equations~\eqref{eq:filt_C} and~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} deliver $C_\mu$,
whereas a calorimeter works at fixed particle number. The conversion is the boxed
formula of Section~\ref{sec:caveat},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_CN}
C_N=C_\mu-T\,
\frac{\big[(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}\big]^{2}}
     {(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}}\,,
\end{equation}
and the kernels of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_kernels} say at once how large the
correction is. By fact (ii) the numerator is an \emph{odd} moment of $\rho$ about
$\mu$; for $\rho$ smooth on the scale $k_BT$, expanding
$\rho(\mu+2k_BTx)\simeq\rho(\mu)+2k_BTx\,\rho'(\mu)$ kills the leading term and
leaves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_dNdT}
\left(\frac{\partial N}{\partial T}\right)_{\!V,\mu}
\simeq 2k_B^{2}T\rho'(\mu)\!\int\! x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x\,dx
=\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}k_B^{2}T\,\rho'(\mu),
\end{equation}
the standard Sommerfeld result, obtained here as the statement that an odd kernel
sees only the slope. With
$(\partial N/\partial\mu)_T\simeq\rho(\mu)$ and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sommerfeld}, the relative correction is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_ratio}
\boxed{\;\frac{C_\mu-C_N}{C_\mu}\;\simeq\;
\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\left(k_BT\,\frac{d\ln\rho}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\mu}\right)^{\!2}\;}
\end{equation}
This is the useful form. The correction is second order in the single
dimensionless group $k_BT\,\rho'/\rho$, i.e.\ in the ratio of the kernel width to
the scale on which $\rho$ varies. For an ordinary metal, where $\rho$ varies on
the scale of the bandwidth $W$ and $k_BT/W\sim10^{-2}$, it is of order $10^{-4}$
and the distinction between $C_\mu$ and $C_N$ is invisible. It ceases to be
negligible exactly when $\rho$ acquires structure on the scale $k_BT$ --- a van
Hove singularity at the Fermi level, a pseudogap, a narrow impurity resonance ---
which is also the regime in which $c_V$ is being measured for what that structure
says. The correction is largest where it is least convenient to forget, and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_ratio} shows why the two heat capacities of
Section~\ref{sec:caveat} are not a bookkeeping nicety.

\subsection{A power-law density of states}
\label{sec:filt_power}

The value of the filter form is that scale covariance --- fact (i) --- turns a
scale-free spectrum into an exponent by inspection. Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_powerlaw}
\rho(\varepsilon)=A\,|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r},\qquad r>-1,
\end{equation}
over a window wide compared with $k_BT$; $r=0$ is a flat band, $r=1$ a Dirac
point in two dimensions, $r=2$ in three, $-1<r<0$ a divergent van Hove-like
enhancement. Substituting into Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} with
$\varepsilon-\mu=2k_BTx$ and $d\varepsilon=2k_BT\,dx$, the temperature comes out
of the integral entirely:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_power_C}
C_\mu=k_B A\,(2k_BT)^{r+1}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!|x|^{r}
x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x\,dx
\;=\;2k_B A\,(k_BT)^{r+1}\,\Gamma(r+3)\,\eta(r+2),
\end{equation}
where the moment integral is evaluated by expanding
$\operatorname{sech}^{2}x=4\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n-1}n\,e^{-2nx}$ term by term for
$x>0$, giving $\int_0^\infty x^{s-1}\operatorname{sech}^2x\,dx=
4\,\Gamma(s)\,\eta(s-1)/2^{s}$ with $\eta(s)=(1-2^{1-s})\zeta(s)$ the Dirichlet
eta function. At $r=0$ this returns
$2k_BA\,k_BT\,\Gamma(3)\eta(2)=\tfrac{\pi^2}{3}k_B^{2}AT$, i.e.\
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sommerfeld}. So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_exponent}
\boxed{\;\rho\sim|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r}\;\Longrightarrow\;C_\mu\sim T^{\,r+1},
\qquad \frac{C_\mu}{T}\sim T^{\,r}\;}
\end{equation}
The entropy carries the \emph{same} exponent with a different amplitude, and the
ratio is exact. Integrating $\int_0^\infty x^{r}w_S\,dx$ by parts with
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sprime} gives
$\int_0^\infty x^{r}w_S\,dx=(r+1)^{-1}k_B\int_0^\infty x^{r+2}
\operatorname{sech}^2x\,dx$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_SC}
\boxed{\;\frac{S}{C_\mu}=\frac{1}{r+1}\qquad\text{exactly, for }
\rho\propto|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r}\;}
\end{equation}
The Sommerfeld coincidence $S=C_\mu$ is the special case $r=0$ and nothing more.
Three readings of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_SC} are worth having.

For $r>0$ (a semimetal) $S<C_\mu$: states are scarce at $\mu$, and the few that
the window catches are far enough out to be heavily energy weighted.

For $-1<r<0$ the Sommerfeld ratio $C_\mu/T\sim T^{r}$ \emph{diverges} as
$T\to0$, while $C_\mu$ itself still vanishes. This is worth separating, because
the two are routinely conflated: a diverging $\gamma=C/T$ is a statement about
the enhancement of the density of states at $\mu$, not about a divergent heat
capacity, and it is entirely compatible with the third law.

As $r\to-1^{+}$, $S/C_\mu\to\infty$: the entropy stops vanishing as $T\to0$ and
tends to a residual value fixed by the lower cutoff of the scale-free window.
For $r\le-1$ the integral $\int d\varepsilon\,\rho$ diverges at $\mu$: there would
be infinitely many thermally active levels at arbitrarily low energy, and both
the third law and the normalisability of $\rho$ fail. The realisable extreme is
therefore $r\to-1$, i.e.\ a residual entropy, and not a divergent $C$.

\subsection{Reading the shape of \texorpdfstring{$c(T)$}{c(T)}}
\label{sec:filt_shape}

The elementary reading of the specific heat is calorimetric: $c=\dbar Q/dT$, so
$\Delta T\simeq\Delta Q/c$, and a large $c$ means that a given amount of heat
raises the temperature little because the system has somewhere to put it. The
filter form makes this quantitative and corrects it in one respect: ``somewhere
to put it'' is neither the total number of states nor the number below $k_BT$,
but the number that $w_c$ actually weighs. With that correction, the standard
readings of the \emph{shape} of $c(T)$ follow, and they are worth listing because
the practical use of a measured $c(T)$ consists in running them backwards.

\paragraph{Rise.} An increase of $c$ with temperature means that the kernel is
dilating into a region of higher level density, so that each further unit of
temperature activates more excitations than the last. Equation~\eqref{eq:filt_exponent}
is the quantitative version: a pure power $c\sim T^{r+1}$ is the case in which the
spectrum presents no scale for the dilation to encounter, and the measured
exponent returns $r$ directly.

\paragraph{Plateau.} A temperature-independent $c$ is the marginal case
$r+1=0$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_exponent}, i.e.\ $\rho\propto|\varepsilon-\mu|^{-1}$,
for which the power-law integrals fail logarithmically and the level count in a
window of width $\Delta$ grows as $\ln\Delta$ rather than as a power. Since
Section~\ref{sec:filt_power} showed $r\le-1$ to be non-normalisable, a plateau
cannot extend to $T\to0$: it is necessarily the behaviour \emph{between} the two
cutoffs of a scale-free window, which is what a geometric hierarchy of levels
produces, and it terminates at the bottom in the residual entropy that the lower
cutoff fixes. A plateau is thus considerably more informative than it looks --- it
identifies logarithmic counting, and it comes with a prediction for where it must
end.

\paragraph{Peaks, and the staircase caveat.} A peak is the generic shape whenever
the spectrum has a largest relevant scale. Its rising side is the filling of the
window; its falling side is \emph{exhaustion} --- once $k_BT$ exceeds the largest
splitting in the group of levels concerned, they are all equally undecided, their
occupations stop changing with temperature, and by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_product} the sector ceases to absorb heat. The bookkeeping is
the entropy sum rule, which follows immediately from $C=T(\partial S/\partial T)$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_sumrule}
\int_0^{\infty}C(T)\,\frac{dT}{T}=S(\infty)-S(0).
\end{equation}
The area under $c/T$ \emph{counts the states released}, and a peak is the only
way a finite count can be released over a finite range.

The caveat concerns the frequent claim that a tiered spectrum gives a staircase
in $c$. It does not. A set of \emph{well-separated} tiers gives a staircase in
$S(T)$, whose step heights are the logarithms of the tier multiplicities, and a
\emph{sequence of peaks} in $c(T)$, one per tier, with $c$ returning towards zero
between them. A staircase in $c$ requires the tiers to overlap on a logarithmic
temperature axis, i.e.\ to be dense enough to produce the logarithmic counting of
the previous paragraph.

\paragraph{High temperature.} Whether $c$ must vanish as $T\to\infty$ is settled
by the same sum rule, and the answer is conditional. If the spectrum is bounded
and the degrees of freedom have finite dimension, then $S(\infty)$ is bounded by
$k_B\ln(\dim\mathcal{H})$ and Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sumrule} forces $c\to0$; the
explicit form is the Schottky tail obtained from Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C} at
$x_\lambda\to0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_schottky}
C_\mu\;\xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}\;k_B\sum_\lambda x_\lambda^{2}
=\frac{\Var_\infty(E-\mu N)}{k_BT^{2}}\;\propto\;\frac{1}{T^{2}},
\end{equation}
with a finite coefficient. Degrees of freedom of unbounded spectrum and unbounded
occupation --- harmonic modes, which fall outside the two-level decomposition of
Section~\ref{sec:filt_twolevel} --- instead settle at the equipartition value
$k_B$ per mode, with $S$ growing as $\ln T$ without bound. A vanishing
high-temperature $c$ is thus a statement about the finite dimension of the local
Hilbert space, not a thermodynamic requirement.

\paragraph{Divergence.} This is the informative case, and the filter form settles
what it can and cannot mean. Since Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C} is a sum of terms each
bounded by Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_peak}, the bound holds term by term:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_bound}
\boxed{\;C_\mu\;\leq\;0.4393\,k_B\times(\text{number of orbitals})\;}
\end{equation}
at \emph{every} temperature. A lattice model with finitely many orbitals per site
and a temperature-independent spectrum therefore has an $\mathcal{O}(k_B)$ heat
capacity per site and cannot diverge at all. Every divergence is consequently a
statement that the spectrum itself \emph{moves}: with temperature, through
self-consistency --- an order parameter that reconstructs the band as it
re-equilibrates --- or with a control parameter, through the accumulation of
level crossings at a transition. And a divergence as $T\to0$ is excluded on the
second, independent ground of Section~\ref{sec:filt_power}: it would require
$r<-1$, hence a non-normalisable $\rho$ and a violation of the third law. What
diverges in a non-Fermi-liquid regime is the Sommerfeld ratio $C/T\sim T^{r}$,
not $C$.

\begin{remark}
Equation~\eqref{eq:filt_bound} is a statement about non-interacting levels, and
the escape clause in it --- ``temperature-independent spectrum'' --- is where all
the interesting physics of strongly correlated systems lives. It is nevertheless
the right null hypothesis: any claim of an anomalously large $c$ carries the
burden of identifying either the extra orbitals or the moving spectrum that
produces it.
\end{remark}

\section{Summary}

\[
\boxed{\;\text{The natural potential is fixed by the variables the environment
holds fixed.}\;}
\]
\[
\boxed{\;\text{Potential}=-k_BT\ln(\text{partition function});\quad
\text{2nd derivative}=\text{response}=\text{variance.}\;}
\]
\[
\boxed{\;\text{At fixed natural variables, the potential is minimised over the
internal variables.}\;}
\]
And the caveat worth carrying away: a response function is labelled not only by
what is differentiated but by everything held fixed while differentiating. Fixed
$\mu$ and fixed $N$ give genuinely different heat capacities, related by the
conversion formula of Section~\ref{sec:caveat}, even though the ensembles agree
on every potential.

\appendix

\section{Total differentiability and its failure}
\label{app:diff}

This appendix supports assumption (A4) of Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}: existence
of all partial derivatives, and even of all directional derivatives, does not
imply that a function has a differential.

\paragraph{Landau symbols.} Let $g(\mathbf{h})>0$ (here $g=|\mathbf{h}|$) and let
$\mathbf{h}\to0$. Then $r=O(g)$, ``at most of order $g$'', means $|r|\leq Cg$ for
some constant $C$ and all small $|\mathbf{h}|$: an upper bound, nothing more.
$r=o(g)$, ``of smaller order than $g$'', means $r/g\to0$; equivalently, for
\emph{every} $\epsilon>0$ one has $|r|\leq\epsilon g$ for all sufficiently small
$|\mathbf{h}|$. And $r=\Theta(g)$, ``exactly of order $g$'', means $c\,g\leq
|r|\leq Cg$ for constants $0<c\leq C$: upper \emph{and} lower bounds, so $r/g$
neither diverges nor tends to zero. The relations among them are $o(g)\subset
O(g)$ and $\Theta(g)\subset O(g)$, but crucially $\Theta(g)\cap o(g)=\emptyset$,
and it is this disjointness that does the work below. With $g=|h|$ in one
variable: $h^{2}=o(|h|)$; $3h=\Theta(|h|)$, hence $O(|h|)$ but not $o(|h|)$;
$|h|^{3/2}=o(|h|)$; and $h\sin(1/h)=O(|h|)$ but is neither $o(|h|)$, the ratio
oscillating, nor $\Theta(|h|)$, since it vanishes at $h=1/n\pi$. In more than one
variable the constants must be uniform in the direction of approach, and it is
exactly that uniformity clause which fails below.

\paragraph{The counterexample.} Consider
\[
f(x,y)=\frac{x^{3}}{x^{2}+y^{2}},\qquad f(0,0)=0 .
\]
In polar form $f=r\cos^{3}\theta$, so $f$ is continuous everywhere; both partial
derivatives exist everywhere, with $f_x(0,0)=1$ and $f_y(0,0)=0$. Yet the
proposed linear approximation fails at the origin:
\[
r(h,k)\;\equiv\;f(h,k)-\big[f(0,0)+1\cdot h+0\cdot k\big]
=-\frac{hk^{2}}{h^{2}+k^{2}} .
\]
Differentiability demands $r(\mathbf{h})=o(|\mathbf{h}|)$, i.e.\ that
$r(\mathbf{h})/|\mathbf{h}|\to0$. It does not: along $h=k$ one has $r=-h/2$ while
$|\mathbf{h}|=\sqrt{2}|h|$, so the ratio is the nonzero constant
$\mp1/(2\sqrt{2})$ as $h\to0^{\pm}$.

\paragraph{The same fact in terms of directional derivatives.} Along the ray
$\mathbf{h}=t\,\hat{\mathbf{u}}$ with
$\hat{\mathbf{u}}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)$,
\[
f(t\cos\theta,\,t\sin\theta)=t\cos^{3}\theta
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
D_{\hat{\mathbf{u}}}f(0)=\cos^{3}\theta .
\]
So every directional derivative exists; there is nothing pathological about the
individual limits. But if $f$ were differentiable at the origin with gradient
$(1,0)$, the directional derivative would have to be the \emph{linear} function
of direction $\nabla f\cdot\hat{\mathbf{u}}=\cos\theta$, and
$\cos^{3}\theta\neq\cos\theta$.

The trouble is not that the derivative depends on direction: for every
non-constant differentiable function it does, through $\nabla
f\cdot\hat{\mathbf{u}}$. The trouble is that it depends on direction
\emph{non-linearly}. Differentiability is precisely the assertion that
$\hat{\mathbf{u}}\mapsto D_{\hat{\mathbf{u}}}f$ is linear --- equivalently, that
it is contraction with a single covector, which is the geometric restatement in
Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}. A function whose directional derivatives fail to be
linear in the direction has no gradient, no differential and no tangent plane,
however many directional derivatives exist individually.

The two descriptions are one calculation. Along the ray,
$r(t\hat{\mathbf{u}})=t\big(D_{\hat{\mathbf{u}}}f-\nabla
f\cdot\hat{\mathbf{u}}\big)+o(t)$, so
\[
\frac{r(\mathbf{h})}{|\mathbf{h}|}\;\xrightarrow[t\to0^{+}]{}\;
\cos^{3}\theta-\cos\theta=-\cos\theta\,\sin^{2}\theta ,
\]
which at $\theta=45^{\circ}$ equals $-1/(2\sqrt{2})$, exactly the constant found
along $h=k$. The remainder fails to be $o(|\mathbf{h}|)$ in a given direction by
precisely the amount by which $D_{\hat{\mathbf{u}}}f$ fails to be linear in
$\hat{\mathbf{u}}$.

One caution on the notation: observing that $r=O(|\mathbf{h}|)$ settles nothing,
because $o(|\mathbf{h}|)\subset O(|\mathbf{h}|)$ --- every admissible remainder is
also $O(|\mathbf{h}|)$. What excludes differentiability is that $r$ is
$\Theta(|\mathbf{h}|)$ along some direction; the \emph{lower} bound is the
operative half. The culprit is visible in $f_x$, which tends to $1$ along $y=0$
and to $0$ along $x=0$: the partials exist but are discontinuous, so $f$ is not
$C^{1}$ (footnote~\ref{fn:Cn}).

\section{Uniqueness of an expansion, and the intensive triple}
\label{app:basis}

The state manifold supplies six one-forms of interest --- $dS,dV,dN,dT,dP,d\mu$
--- in a cotangent space of dimension three, so they are certainly not all
linearly independent. How can an expansion in them be unique? The answer is that
one never expands in all of them: uniqueness of coefficients is a property of a
\emph{basis}, and the general statement is linear algebra.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Non-uniqueness.} If $\omega=a\,dT+b\,dP+c\,d\mu$, then equally
\[
\omega=(a+\lambda S)\,dT+(b-\lambda V)\,dP+(c+\lambda N)\,d\mu
\qquad\text{for every }\lambda\in\mathbb{R},
\]
since the added piece is $\lambda(S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu)=0$ by Gibbs--Duhem. The
coefficients are pinned down only up to a one-parameter family --- which is
precisely why a symbol such as $(\partial\,\cdot/\partial T)_{P,\mu}$ has no
meaning.
\item \textbf{Non-existence.} The span $W$ of $\{dT,dP,d\mu\}$ is only
two-dimensional, so a covector outside it admits no expansion whatever. $dS$ is
such a covector.
\end{itemize}

The geometric reason for both is that $T,P,\mu$ are homogeneous of degree
\emph{zero}, hence constant along the scaling direction
\[
X_E\;=\;S\,\partial_S+V\,\partial_V+N\,\partial_N .
\]
Differentiating $T(\lambda S,\lambda V,\lambda N)=T(S,V,N)$ at $\lambda=1$ gives
$dT(X_E)=0$, and likewise for $P$ and $\mu$. All three one-forms therefore lie in
the \emph{annihilator} of $X_E$ --- the set $\{\omega\in
T^{*}_p\mathcal{M}:\omega(X_E)=0\}$ of covectors that return zero on $X_E$, which
for $X_E\neq0$ is a linear subspace of dimension $3-1=2$. Equivalently, the map
$p\mapsto\big(T(p),P(p),\mu(p)\big)$ has rank two and its image is the
Gibbs--Duhem surface, and no three one-forms pulled back from a two-dimensional
image can span three dimensions. By contrast $dS(X_E)=S\neq0$, so $dS$ is not in
the annihilator and indeed has no expansion in $dT,dP,d\mu$.

So (A3) asks exactly what it says: that the three coordinate one-forms of the
chart actually in use be linearly independent. Any independent triple serves ---
$\{dS,dV,dN\}$, $\{dT,dV,dN\}$ and $\{dT,dP,dN\}$ are all bases, being the
coordinate one-forms of the charts natural to $U$, $F$ and $G$ --- and each gives
its own unique but \emph{different} coefficients for one and the same one-form.
That is all a change of variables is, and the expansion of $S\,dT$ in
$\{dS,dV,dN\}$ used in Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff} is of exactly this kind.

\section{When extensivity fails}
\label{app:extensivity}

Euler's relation assumes strict extensivity, and the criterion is worth stating
in a form that does not presuppose a classical geometric picture.
Surface-to-volume language is a special case, not the definition: in a quantum
many-body problem one has a Hamiltonian on a Hilbert space, and ``the area of the
interface'' is not a primitive notion there.

The criterion is a statement about how the energy scales with the number of
constituents. Extensivity holds if and only if
\[
u\;\equiv\;\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\langle\hat H\rangle}{N}
\qquad\text{at fixed densities}
\]
exists and is finite, i.e.\ if $\langle\hat H\rangle=uN+o(N)$, and likewise for
$S$ and $\ln Z$. ``At fixed densities'' means that the limit is taken with every
intensive ratio held constant --- the number density $N/V$, the energy density
$E/V$, and the concentration of each species --- so that only the overall size of
the sample grows. Everything else follows, because Euler's relation is nothing
but the degree-one homogeneity that $o(N)$ corrections cannot spoil. Three
regimes:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Short-range interactions, large $N$.} The correction is a boundary
term, and what it counts is \emph{constituents}, not area: the particles whose
local environment differs from the bulk are those within a correlation length
$\xi$ of the boundary, and for a $d$-dimensional sample of linear size $L$ their
fraction is $N_{\rm bdy}/N\sim\xi/L\sim N^{-1/d}\to0$. That is where the
``surface/volume'' slogan comes from. The geometric phrasing is a convenient proxy
available in a solid; the counting phrasing survives when it is not.
\item \textbf{Small $N$.} If $N^{-1/d}$ is not small there is no separation to
exploit and Euler's relation is simply inaccurate: a $3\,\mathrm{nm}$
nanoparticle has of order $10\%$ of its atoms in a distinct environment, a film
of thickness $t$ has $N_{\rm bdy}/N\sim a/t$ with $a$ a lattice constant, and a
few-site cluster has no bulk at all. The corrections are then not error terms but
the physics: surface tension, surface stress and Tolman-type curvature
corrections are exactly these $o(N)$ terms promoted to observables, obtained by
comparing $\langle\hat H\rangle$ for the finite system with $uN$ --- a
subtraction requiring no geometric area.
\item \textbf{Long-range interactions.} Here the failure is qualitative and no
boundary language applies. For a pair potential decaying as $r^{-\sigma}$ with
$\sigma\leq d$ the interaction energy per particle grows without bound; for
unscreened Coulomb or Newtonian gravity the total energy scales as $N^{2}$, so
$\langle\hat H\rangle/N$ diverges and the limit above does not exist at all.
Euler's relation, Gibbs--Duhem and $\Omega=-PV$ fail together, and the
microcanonical entropy can be genuinely non-concave in the thermodynamic limit
(Appendix~\ref{app:fisher}).
\end{itemize}

\section{Critical exponents: sign, shape, and universality}
\label{app:cusp}

\subsection*{Divergence versus cusp}

Reading $c_{h}\sim|t|^{-\alpha}$ requires care with signs, because the two
possibilities look nearly identical on paper and are qualitatively different in
fact. The exponent is defined so that $c$ carries $|t|^{-\alpha}$; hence
\begin{itemize}
\item $\alpha>0$: $|t|^{-\alpha}\to+\infty$ as $t\to0$, so $c$ \emph{diverges}.
The divergence is weak when $\alpha$ is small, but $c$ has no finite limit.
\item $\alpha=0$: the power law degenerates into $c\sim-\tilde A\ln|t|$, which
also diverges. This is the two-dimensional Ising case computed in
Section~\ref{sec:critical}.
\item $\alpha<0$: writing $\alpha=-|\alpha|$ gives
$|t|^{-\alpha}=|t|^{|\alpha|}\to0$, so the singular part \emph{vanishes} at $t=0$
and $c$ approaches a finite value. Because $|t|^{|\alpha|}$ has infinite slope at
the origin for $0<|\alpha|<1$, it approaches that value with a vertical tangent:
a \emph{cusp}, finite peak with infinite slope.
\end{itemize}
The standard parametrisation covering all three at once is
\[
c_{h}=\frac{A_{\pm}}{\alpha}\Big(|t|^{-\alpha}-1\Big)+B ,
\]
divergent for $\alpha>0$, tending to $B-A_{\pm}/\alpha$ with infinite slope for
$\alpha<0$, and reducing to $-A_{\pm}\ln|t|+B$ as $\alpha\to0$ by L'H\^opital.
Figure~\ref{fig:cusp} shows the three shapes.

So the difference between the three-dimensional Ising class,
$\alpha\simeq+0.110$, and the three-dimensional XY class describing the
$\lambda$ transition of ${}^{4}$He, $\alpha\simeq-0.013$, is purely a difference
of \emph{sign}, and it is a real one: the former has $c\to\infty$ at $T_c$, the
latter a finite $c$ at $T_\lambda$ reached with infinite slope.

Both are experimentally brutal. With $\alpha=0.110$ the factor $|t|^{-\alpha}$
has reached only $2.8$ at $|t|=10^{-4}$ and $4.6$ at $|t|=10^{-6}$: a divergence
that has not quite quintupled after six decades in $t$. With $\alpha=-0.013$ the
singular factor has fallen only to $0.84$ at $|t|=10^{-6}$. Separating them
demands temperature control at the nanokelvin level and careful subtraction of the
regular background $B$; the ${}^{4}$He measurement was flown in microgravity to
remove the gravitational rounding of $T_\lambda$ across the height of the sample.
A numerically small exponent is hard to measure. It is not thereby ill-defined.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,x=2.85cm,y=0.60cm]
  \draw[->] (-1.15,0) -- (1.24,0);
  \node[anchor=north east,inner sep=2pt] at (1.24,-0.18) {$t=(T-T_c)/T_c$};
  \draw[->] (-1.15,0) -- (-1.15,4.55) node[left]{$c$};
  \draw[dotted,gray] (0,0) -- (0,4.40);
  \draw[very thick,teal!65!black,samples=140,domain=-1:1,variable=\x,smooth]
       plot ({\x},{1+0.7*(1-sqrt(abs(\x)))});
  \draw[very thick,blue!60!black,samples=140,domain=0.0035:1,variable=\x,smooth]
       plot ({\x},{1-0.35*ln(abs(\x))});
  \draw[very thick,blue!60!black,samples=140,domain=-1:-0.0035,variable=\x,smooth]
       plot ({\x},{1-0.35*ln(abs(\x))});
  \draw[very thick,red!70!black,samples=140,domain=0.043:1,variable=\x,smooth]
       plot ({\x},{1+0.7*(pow(abs(\x),-0.5)-1)});
  \draw[very thick,red!70!black,samples=140,domain=-1:-0.043,variable=\x,smooth]
       plot ({\x},{1+0.7*(pow(abs(\x),-0.5)-1)});
  \node[anchor=north,font=\scriptsize] at (0,-0.10) {$T_c$};
  \draw[very thick,red!70!black]  (-1.09,4.25) -- (-0.94,4.25);
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1.5pt] at (-0.92,4.25) {$\alpha>0$: diverges};
  \draw[very thick,blue!60!black] (-1.09,3.70) -- (-0.94,3.70);
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1.5pt] at (-0.92,3.70) {$\alpha=0$: $\log$};
  \draw[very thick,teal!65!black] (-1.09,3.15) -- (-0.94,3.15);
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1.5pt] at (-0.92,3.15) {$\alpha<0$: cusp};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The three behaviours of
$c_{h}=(A_{\pm}/\alpha)\big(|t|^{-\alpha}-1\big)+B$, drawn with the same
amplitude and background for each. Only the \emph{sign} of $\alpha$ matters:
$\alpha>0$ gives a genuine divergence (red, here $\alpha=\tfrac12$, truncated at
the top of the frame), $\alpha=0$ the logarithm of the 2D Ising model (blue), and
$\alpha<0$ a finite peak approached with infinite slope --- a cusp (teal, here
$\alpha=-\tfrac12$). The exponents drawn are an order of magnitude larger than
the physical ones ($\alpha\simeq+0.110$ for 3D Ising, $\alpha\simeq-0.013$ for
3D XY), which is why the distinction, though sharp in principle, is so
demanding to establish in the laboratory.}
\label{fig:cusp}
\end{figure}

\subsection*{What universality does and does not deliver}

The exponents are often oversold and then dismissed, so it is worth being
explicit.

The content is stronger than ``the same exponents''. Systems in one class share
the scaling function $\Phi$, the amplitude ratios built from it
($A_+/A_-$, $\Gamma_+/\Gamma_-$, $R_\chi$, the universal Binder value
$U_4^{\ast}$), and the correction exponent $\omega$ --- everything except two
scale factors and $T_c$. That is a sharp, falsifiable claim, and it is why a
lattice model with no chemistry in it can be used to predict the critical
behaviour of a real fluid.

What the class label buys physically is the identification of what is
\emph{relevant}. Class membership is fixed by dimensionality, the symmetry of the
order parameter and the range of the interactions, so measuring the class
determines those --- and those are microscopic statements. Distinguishing Ising
from XY from Heisenberg exponents in a magnet identifies the anisotropy of the
exchange; a crossover from three- to two-dimensional exponents in a layered
material measures the onset of decoupling; a shift to random-fixed-point
exponents diagnoses whether disorder is relevant. The negative statement matters
equally: lattice structure, the detailed potential and the band structure are
\emph{irrelevant} near $T_c$, which is what licenses coarse-grained modelling at
all.

What it does not buy is equally definite. The class determines no amplitude, no
$T_c$ and no quantity away from the critical region, so two systems in one class
have entirely different equations of state at ordinary temperatures; and it gives
no route back to the microscopic Hamiltonian, the map from Hamiltonians to
classes being many-to-one by construction. The exponents are also experimentally
demanding: with $\alpha\simeq0.11$ the factor $|t|^{-\alpha}$ has reached only
$2.8$ at $|t|=10^{-4}$, and must be separated from a comparable analytic
background over several decades in $t$. The ${}^{4}$He $\lambda$ transition,
measured in microgravity to remove the gravitational rounding of $T_\lambda$
across the sample, gives $\alpha=-0.0127(3)$: a precise measurement, and one that
disagrees with the best lattice and field-theory values near $-0.015$ by several
standard deviations. That discrepancy is unresolved. The lesson is that these
exponents are hard, not that they are ill-defined.

\section{Exact and inexact differentials}
\label{app:exact}

A state function has an exact differential,
\[
df=
\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right)_y dx+
\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\right)_x dy,
\]
so that $\int_1^2 df=f_2-f_1$ and $\oint df=0$: the integral depends only on the
endpoints. Heat and work are transfer processes, not properties of a state, and
their one-forms are inexact:
\[
\oint\dbar Q\neq 0,\qquad \oint\dbar W\neq 0 .
\]
A heat engine is precisely a device exploiting $\oint\dbar Q\neq0$.

\paragraph{How a one-form can fail to be the differential of anything.} The
notation invites the objection that $\dbar Q$ ``is just like $dU$''. It is not,
and the difference is checkable. $\dbar Q=dU-\dbar W=dU+P\,dV$ (at fixed $N$) is
a perfectly ordinary one-form: at each point of the state manifold it is a linear
functional on tangent vectors, defined by that formula. Being a one-form and
being \emph{the differential of a function} are different properties, and only
the second is what $d$ asserts. On a simply connected domain a one-form
$\omega=A\,dx+B\,dy$ is exact --- i.e.\ $\omega=df$ for some $f$ --- if and only
if
\[
\boxed{\;\frac{\partial A}{\partial y}=\frac{\partial B}{\partial x}\;}
\]
which is just the equality of mixed second partials of the hypothetical $f$.

Apply the test to $\dbar Q$ in the chart $(T,V)$. Since
$dU=C_V\,dT+(\partial U/\partial V)_T\,dV$,
\[
\dbar Q=C_V\,dT+\left[\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_T+P\right]dV ,
\]
so exactness would require
\[
\left(\frac{\partial C_V}{\partial V}\right)_T
=\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial T\,\partial V}
+\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V .
\]
The left side is $\partial^{2}U/\partial V\,\partial T$, equal to the first term
on the right, so the condition collapses to $(\partial P/\partial T)_V=0$ --- false
for every real substance. There is therefore no function $Q$ of state whose
differential is $\dbar Q$, and the notation records that fact. The same
conclusion follows directly from $\oint\dbar Q\neq0$ around a Carnot cycle. By
contrast $dU$ passes the test identically, because $U$ exists first and the
one-form is built from it.

\paragraph{Maxwell relations.} Applied to $dU$, $dF$, $dG$, $d\Omega$, which
\emph{are} exact, the same condition generates the Maxwell relations. From
$dF=-S\,dT-P\,dV$ and $dG=-S\,dT+V\,dP$,
\[
\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
=\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N},
\qquad
\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial P}\right)_{T,N}
=-\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N}.
\]
These are the workhorses that convert an unmeasurable entropy derivative into a
measurable equation-of-state derivative. They require $F,G\in C^{2}$, which is
the symmetry of the Hessian (footnote~\ref{fn:Cn}).

\paragraph{Integrating factor.} Although $\dbar Q_{\rm rev}$ is inexact, dividing
by $T$ makes it exact:
\[
\boxed{\;\frac{\dbar Q_{\rm rev}}{T}=dS\;}
\]
so $1/T$ is an \emph{integrating factor}: a function $\lambda$ such that
$\lambda\omega$ is exact even though $\omega$ is not. That such a factor exists
at all is the content of Carath\'eodory's formulation of the second law --- in
any neighbourhood of any equilibrium state there are states unreachable by
adiabatic processes, which forces $\dbar Q_{\rm rev}$ to admit an integrating
factor --- and the universality of $T$ follows from consistency between coupled
systems.

\section{Glasses and the scope of the formalism}
\label{app:glass}

The frequency-dependent specific heat of a glass former can leave the impression
that glasses are outside statistical mechanics. They are not, but which parts of
the apparatus survive is worth separating.

\emph{Statistical mechanics survives untouched.} A glass is an ordinary
Hamiltonian system: the microstates, the phase-space measure and the Boltzmann
weight are exactly what they always were. What is lost is \emph{ergodicity on the
experimental timescale}: the trajectory explores only one basin of the energy
landscape, so time averages stop equalling full ensemble averages. The standard
repair is to average over the region actually visited --- a \emph{restricted
ensemble} --- which is the same device used for a ferromagnet below $T_c$, where
one does not average over both magnetisation directions. The difference is only
that the restriction is imposed by kinetics rather than by symmetry.

\emph{Equilibrium thermodynamics survives in the constrained sense.} Below $T_g$
the glass is in constrained equilibrium at a frozen configurational coordinate,
which is the second regime of assumption (A2) in Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}. All
state functions exist as functions of $(S,V,N;\xi)$, and the practical
bookkeeping device is the \emph{fictive temperature} $T_f$, the temperature at
which the frozen structure would be the equilibrium one; measured properties then
depend on $T$ through the fast degrees of freedom and on $T_f$ through the frozen
ones. The residual entropy at $T\to0$ --- the configurational entropy of the
basin multiplicity --- is a genuine, measurable quantity, obtained by comparing
the calorimetric entropy of the glass with that of the crystal. It is not a
violation of the third law but a statement that the constrained system has more
than one accessible ground state.

\emph{What genuinely fails.} Three things. (i) The identification $\dbar
Q=T\,dS$, whenever the measurement timescale is comparable to $\tau_\xi$; this is
why $c(\omega)$ is complex. (ii) The fluctuation--dissipation theorem in its
equilibrium form: response and correlation are no longer related by a single
temperature, and the deviation is parametrised by an effective temperature
$T_{\rm eff}(\omega)>T$ characterising the slow modes. (iii) Uniqueness of the
state: properties depend on preparation history (cooling rate, ageing time), so
there is no function of $(T,P)$ alone to tabulate.

\section{Non-convexity and the Maxwell construction}
\label{app:maxwell}

\paragraph{Convexity of the exact free energy.}

For a system with short-range interactions the exact $F(T,V,N)$ is convex in $V$.
The argument is physical and requires no calculation: given a container of volume
$V=xV_1+(1-x)V_2$, the system may always \emph{phase-separate} into a fraction $x$
at volume $V_1$ and a fraction $1-x$ at volume $V_2$, ignoring the interface. That
configuration has free energy $xF(V_1)+(1-x)F(V_2)$, so the true minimum satisfies
\[
F\big(xV_1+(1-x)V_2\big)\;\leq\;xF(V_1)+(1-x)F(V_2),
\]
which is convexity. Therefore any non-convexity in a computed $F(V)$ is an
artifact of the approximation used --- specifically, of a mean-field or
constrained calculation that implicitly forbids phase separation by assuming a
spatially uniform state.

\paragraph{The double-tangent construction.}

The van der Waals equation is the standard illustration. Below $T_c$ its isotherm
$P(V)$ contains a region with $(\partial P/\partial V)_T>0$, which is mechanically
unstable, and correspondingly $F(V)$ has a region where
$\partial^2F/\partial V^2=-(\partial P/\partial V)_T<0$, i.e.\ non-convex. The
repair is to replace $F$ by its convex hull, which on the offending interval is a
straight line --- the \emph{double tangent} touching the curve at $V_\ell$ and
$V_g$. The two tangency conditions have direct physical meaning:
\[
\underbrace{\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial V}\right|_{V_\ell}
=\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial V}\right|_{V_g}}_{\textstyle P_\ell=P_g\equiv P_0}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\underbrace{F-V\frac{\partial F}{\partial V}\Big|_{V_\ell}
=F-V\frac{\partial F}{\partial V}\Big|_{V_g}}_{\textstyle G_\ell=G_g,\ \text{i.e.}\ \mu_\ell=\mu_g}
\]
Equal slope is mechanical equilibrium, equal intercept is chemical equilibrium
(the intercept of the tangent is $F+PV=G=\mu N$). Together they are exactly the
coexistence conditions.

The straight segment is not fictitious: a point on it at volume
$V=xV_\ell+(1-x)V_g$ is realised by a physical mixture of liquid and gas in
proportions given by the \emph{lever rule}, and its free energy
$xF_\ell+(1-x)F_g$ is linear in $V$ by construction. Because the chord lies
\emph{below} the uniform-state $F(V)$, the system genuinely lowers its free energy
by phase-separating.

\paragraph{Maxwell's equal-area rule.}

The familiar equal-area statement is the same construction in the $P$--$V$ plane.
Along the tangent line,
\[
F(V_g)-F(V_\ell)=-P_0\,(V_g-V_\ell),
\]
while along the van der Waals isotherm
$F(V_g)-F(V_\ell)=-\int_{V_\ell}^{V_g}P_{\rm vdW}\,dV$. Equating,
\[
\boxed{\;\int_{V_\ell}^{V_g}\big[P_{\rm vdW}(V)-P_0\big]\,dV=0\;}
\]
so the horizontal coexistence line $P=P_0$ cuts the loop into two lobes of equal
area. That is Maxwell's rule, and it is nothing but the double-tangent
construction rewritten.

\paragraph{Binodal and spinodal.}

Two curves must be distinguished, and Figure~\ref{fig:binodal} shows them in the
three representations that get used interchangeably. The
\emph{binodal}\footnote{\label{fn:binodal}\textbf{Binodal.} Also called the
\emph{coexistence curve}. It is the locus of the two contact points of the double
tangent --- the two ``nodes'', whence the name --- i.e.\ the pair of states
$(V_\ell,V_g)$, or more generally the pair of compositions, that are in mutual
equilibrium: equal $T$, equal $P$, equal $\mu$. It is what a phase diagram means
by the boundary of the two-phase region. Outside it the equilibrium state is
uniform; inside it the equilibrium state is a \emph{mixture} of the two binodal
states, in proportions fixed by the lever rule, and the free energy is the chord
value rather than the curve value. Operationally the binodal is where a second
phase first appears on slow cooling --- slow enough for nucleation to occur.}
(coexistence curve) is the locus of $V_\ell,V_g$ from the double tangent. The
\emph{spinodal}\footnote{\label{fn:spinodal}\textbf{Spinodal.} The locus where the
curvature of the constrained free energy vanishes:
$\partial^{2}F/\partial V^{2}=0$, equivalently $(\partial P/\partial V)_T=0$, or
$\partial^{2}F/\partial\phi^{2}=0$ for a general order parameter. It is the
boundary of \emph{local} stability, whereas the binodal is the boundary of
\emph{global} stability --- so the spinodal always lies strictly inside the
binodal, the two touching only at the critical point, where $F$ loses its
curvature and the distinction disappears. The physical difference between the two
regions is a difference of decay mechanism. Between binodal and spinodal a
uniform state is stable against small fluctuations but unstable against large
ones, so it must wait for a droplet of the other phase exceeding the critical
nucleus to appear: this is \emph{nucleation}, with a barrier
$\Delta F^{\ast}\propto\sigma^{d}/|\Delta\mu|^{d-1}$ and hence an exponentially
long lifetime --- superheated water, supercooled vapour, retained austenite.
Inside the spinodal $F$ is locally concave, so infinitesimal long-wavelength
fluctuations already lower the free energy; there is no barrier and the uniform
state decomposes immediately at a rate set only by diffusion, producing the
characteristic interconnected morphology of \emph{spinodal decomposition}. One
caveat: unlike the binodal, the spinodal is sharply defined only within mean-field
theory. Fluctuations smear the crossover, the nucleation barrier goes to zero
continuously rather than at a definite locus, and for short-range interactions
there is no exact spinodal at all. It remains indispensable as an
organising concept, and is genuinely sharp in the mean-field limit of long-range
or infinite-dimensional models.} is the locus of inflection points
$\partial^2F/\partial V^2=0$, i.e.\ $(\partial P/\partial V)_T=0$. Between binodal
and spinodal, $F$ is locally convex but above the hull: these are the metastable
states (superheated liquid, supercooled vapour), which decay by nucleation.
Inside the spinodal $F$ is locally concave: these states are absolutely unstable
and decay by spinodal decomposition, with no nucleation barrier.

Finally, a point that causes recurrent confusion: whether one \emph{picks the
lower minimum} of $F(\phi)$ or \emph{takes the convex
hull}\footnote{\label{fn:hull}\textbf{Convex hull of a function.} For a function
$g$ on an interval, $\operatorname{conv}g$ is the largest convex function that
lies nowhere above $g$; equivalently, the pointwise supremum of all affine
functions $\ell$ with $\ell\leq g$, or the lower boundary of the convex hull (in
the ordinary set-theoretic sense) of the region $\{(x,y):y\geq g(x)\}$ above the
graph. Constructively: stretch a taut string underneath the graph and let it pull
tight. Where $g$ is already convex the string lies on the graph and
$\operatorname{conv}g=g$; where it is not, the string bridges the offending piece
as a straight chord touching at two points --- the double tangent, whose contact
points are the binodal (footnote~\ref{fn:binodal}) and whose chord is the tie
line. Two properties are used in the main text: $\operatorname{conv}g$ is the
largest convex minorant, so $\operatorname{conv}g\leq g$ everywhere with equality
off the chord; and $g^{\ast\ast}=\operatorname{conv}g$, i.e.\ applying the
Legendre--Fenchel transform twice returns not $g$ but its convex hull, which is
exactly why transforming to the field ensemble and back performs the Maxwell
construction automatically. Note also what the hull \emph{destroys}: the barrier
between the wells, and with it every metastable and unstable state, since those
lie strictly above the hull.} depends on the ensemble, not on the physics. If the conjugate field is controlled (magnetic
field $h$ fixed), one minimises $F(\phi)-h\phi$ and simply selects the deeper
well. If the order parameter itself is conserved and controlled (density,
alloy composition, total magnetisation), the accessible states include mixtures
and one takes the hull, obtaining a coexistence region. This is the same
Legendre-transform bookkeeping as everywhere else: $U^{\ast\ast}=\operatorname{conv}U$
means that transforming to the field ensemble and back automatically performs the
Maxwell construction and erases the metastable and unstable branches.


\section{Three points of ensemble bookkeeping}
\label{app:bookkeeping}

\subsection*{Entropy at fixed temperature}
The guess that ``$T$ is fixed, so its conjugate $S$ must fluctuate'' is a good
instinct applied to the wrong variable.

What fluctuates is $E$, not $S$. In the canonical ensemble the fixed quantities
are $(T,V,N)$ and the fluctuating one is the energy, with $\Var(E)=k_BT^{2}C_V$.
The conjugate pairing that governs the \emph{ensemble} is $(\beta,E)$: $\beta$ is
the Lagrange multiplier and $E$ the constrained quantity it controls. The pairing
$(T,S)$ belongs to the \emph{potential} --- it says $S=-(\partial F/\partial
T)_{V,N}$ --- and the two need not, and do not, coincide.

Entropy is not an observable. There is no Hermitian operator whose expectation is
the thermodynamic entropy: $S=-k_B\Tr(\rho\ln\rho)$ is a functional of the
distribution, a property of the ensemble as a whole rather than of any
microstate. Asking for its fluctuation is a category error of the same kind as
asking for the fluctuation of the mean of a distribution.

The nearest thing that does fluctuate is the \emph{surprisal}
$s_i\equiv-k_B\ln p_i$, whose ensemble average is $S$ by construction. In the
canonical ensemble $s_i=k_B(\beta E_i+\ln Z)$ is affine in $E_i$, so its
fluctuations are energy fluctuations in disguise:
\[
\Var(s)=k_B^{2}\beta^{2}\Var(E)=k_B^{2}\beta^{2}\,k_BT^{2}C_V=k_B\,C_V .
\]
Its relative spread is then $N^{-1/2}$, and the counting is worth doing
explicitly. Both $C_V$ and $S$ are extensive: at fixed density and temperature
$C_V=N\hat c$ and $S=N\hat s$ with $\hat c,\hat s$ intensive and independent of
$N$. Hence
\[
\frac{\sqrt{\Var(s)}}{S}=\frac{\sqrt{k_BC_V}}{S}
=\frac{\sqrt{k_B N\hat c}}{N\hat s}
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,\frac{\sqrt{k_B\hat c}}{\hat s}\;\propto\;N^{-1/2} .
\]
The $N^{-1/2}$ comes from the mismatch between a standard deviation, which grows
as $\sqrt{N}$ because it is the square root of an extensive variance, and a mean,
which grows as $N$. The same answer comes from the microcanonical route:
treating $S(E)=k_B\ln W(E)$ as a function of the fluctuating $E$ and linearising,
$\delta S\approx\delta E/T$, gives $\Var(S)\approx\Var(E)/T^{2}=k_BC_V$.

So at fixed $T$ the entropy is not a fluctuating quantity whose mean is being
quoted: it is a sharp function of $(T,V,N)$, and the surrogate that can be made
to fluctuate carries no information beyond the energy fluctuation already
accounted for.


\subsection*{Sign of $-\mu N$ in $\Omega$}
Euler's relation carries $+\mu N$ and the grand potential subtracts $\mu N$;
there is no inconsistency, and both signs are forced.

The sign in $\Omega$ is the Legendre convention. A Legendre transform always
\emph{subtracts} the product of the conjugate pair being traded away, whatever
sign that product carries inside $U$: going from $U$ to $F$ one subtracts $TS$
even though $U$ contains $+TS$. The rule is fixed by requiring that the new
potential's differential contain $-N\,d\mu$ rather than $\mu\,dN$, which is the
whole point of the transform.

The sign in the Boltzmann weight is forced by the reservoir's entropy. System and
reservoir together are isolated with total energy $E_{\rm tot}$ and total number
$N_{\rm tot}$; if the system occupies a definite microstate with $(E,N)$, the
number of composite microstates is the reservoir's alone, so
\[
p_{i,N}\ \propto\ W_{\rm res}(E_{\rm tot}-E,\,N_{\rm tot}-N)
=\exp\!\Big[\tfrac{1}{k_B}S_{\rm res}(E_{\rm tot}-E,\,N_{\rm tot}-N)\Big].
\]
Expanding to first order and using $(\partial S/\partial E)_{V,N}=1/T$ and
$(\partial S/\partial N)_{E,V}=-\mu/T$ gives
$p_{i,N}\propto e^{-\beta(E-\mu N)}$. The relative minus sign traces to the fact
that giving the system a particle costs the reservoir energy but relieves it of a
particle. The physics is visible in the result: raising $\mu$ multiplies the
weight of an $N$-particle state by $e^{+\beta\mu N}$ and so favours large $N$,
which is what a large chemical potential is supposed to do. With the opposite
sign a high chemical potential would empty the system.


\subsection*{Worked check: $c_{V,\mu}$ versus $c_{V,N}$ for the ideal gas}
 With
$\Omega=-k_BT\,e^{\beta\mu}V/\lambda_T^{3}$, $\lambda_T\propto T^{-1/2}$ and
$x=\beta\mu$,
\[
C_{V,\mu}=Nk_B\left(\tfrac{15}{4}-3x+x^{2}\right),
\qquad
C_{V,N}=\tfrac{3}{2}Nk_B,
\qquad
C_{V,\mu}-C_{V,N}=Nk_B\left(x-\tfrac{3}{2}\right)^{2}\geq 0 .
\]
The naive grand-canonical answer is not $\tfrac32Nk_B$ and is not even
$\mu$-independent; the correction term restores the familiar result exactly.

\subsection*{Magnetic work: $\mathcal{H}\,dM$ versus $-M\,d\mathcal{H}$}
Both, in different potentials; the pattern is that of $(\mu,N)$ with the total
magnetisation $M$ playing the role of $N$ and the applied field $\mathcal{H}$
that of $\mu$. (The script $\mathcal{H}$ avoids a collision: $H$ is the enthalpy
and $\hat H$ the Hamiltonian. In density notation $h=\mathcal{H}$ and $m=M/V$.)

$M$ is extensive and $\mathcal{H}$ intensive, so $M$ joins $(S,V,N)$ as a natural
variable of $U$ and the work done \emph{on} the system is
$\dbar W=\mathcal{H}\,dM$:
\[
dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN+\mathcal{H}\,dM ,
\]
with $U=TS-PV+\mu N+\mathcal{H}M$ and $S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu+M\,d\mathcal{H}=0$.
Note why $\mathcal{H}\,dM$ carries the opposite sign to $-P\,dV$: magnetising a
sample against a field costs energy, whereas expanding against a pressure
releases it.

An experiment fixes $\mathcal{H}$, not $M$, so one transforms:
\[
\tilde F(T,V,N,\mathcal{H})=F-\mathcal{H}M,
\qquad
d\tilde F=-S\,dT-P\,dV+\mu\,dN-M\,d\mathcal{H} ,
\]
whence $M=-(\partial\tilde F/\partial\mathcal{H})_{T,V,N}$ and
$\chi=-(\partial^{2}\tilde F/\partial\mathcal{H}^{2})_{T,V,N}\geq0$. In the
partition function this is verbatim the construction of $\Xi$,
\[
\mathcal{Z}=\Tr\,e^{-\beta(\hat H-\mathcal{H}\hat M)},
\qquad
\tilde F=-k_BT\ln\mathcal{Z},
\qquad
\Var(M)=k_BT\,\chi ,
\]
parallel to $\Var(N)=k_BT(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}$. So: the
extensive-variable form $\mathcal{H}\,dM$ in $dU$, $dH$, $dF$, $dG$; the
transformed form $-M\,d\mathcal{H}$ in whichever potential has traded $M$ away.
Which is correct is decided by what the apparatus holds fixed.

Two cautions. The division of magnetic work between ``the sample'' and ``the
field in the volume it occupies'' is convention-dependent, and $\mathcal{H}$
versus $B$ versus $\mu_0\mathcal{H}$ differ by exactly such terms; the form above
is the one in which $M$ is the sample's moment and $\mathcal{H}$ the applied
field, which is what experiments report. And ferromagnets are precisely where
hysteresis makes $U$ multivalued --- assumption (A1) --- so these relations hold
on the equilibrium branch, not around a loop.


\section{Energy versus order-parameter histograms}
\label{app:histograms}
Two different histograms are easily conflated, and they behave oppositely.
The modality of each is not a rule of thumb but a consequence of one principle,
so it is worth deriving rather than tabulating.

\paragraph{The principle.} Both histograms are large-deviation distributions of
the form
\[
P(x)\;\propto\;e^{-V\varphi(x)/k_BT} ,
\]
with $\varphi$ a \emph{constrained} free-energy density in the sense of
Section~\ref{sec:minimum}, and $V$ the volume. As $V\to\infty$ the distribution
concentrates on the global minima of $\varphi$, and around each one it is Gaussian
with a width set by the local curvature. Hence
\[
\boxed{\;\#\ \text{peaks of }P(x)\;=\;\#\ \text{degenerate global minima of }
\varphi(x)\;}
\]
Everything below is this statement applied twice, once with $x=E$ and once with
$x=\phi$. Nothing is postulated about ``binary'' order parameters.

\paragraph{$P(E)$: count the distinct energy densities of the coexisting phases.}
Write $E=Ve$ and use $S=k_B\ln W$:
\[
P(e)\;\propto\;W(E)\,e^{-\beta E}
=\exp\!\left[\frac{V}{k_B}\Big(s(e)-\frac{e}{T}\Big)\right],
\]
so $\varphi\propto -\big(s(e)-e/T\big)$ and the maxima solve
\[
s'(e)=\frac{1}{T}
\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad
T(e)=T .
\]
Now use the concavity of $s(e)$ established in Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Away from a first-order transition, $s$ is strictly concave}, so $s'$
is strictly decreasing and $s'(e)=1/T$ has exactly one root. \textbf{One peak, at
every temperature} --- including at $T_c$. Expanding around the root gives
\[
\Var(E)=-\frac{Vk_B}{s''(e^{\ast})}=Vk_BT^{2}c=k_BT^{2}C ,
\]
recovering the canonical result. At a continuous transition $c\to\infty$, so
$s''(e^{\ast})=-1/(T^{2}c)\to0$: the curvature vanishes \emph{at a single point}
without $s$ acquiring a flat interval. A vanishing curvature at one point widens
the peak; only a flat interval can split it. This is the precise sense in which
$P(E)$ ``broadens anomalously but does not split'': $\Var(E)\sim
L^{d+\alpha/\nu}$ rather than $L^{d}$, while the \emph{density} still sharpens,
$\sqrt{\Var(e)}\sim L^{(\alpha/\nu-d)/2}\to0$.
\item \emph{At a first-order transition $s$ has a straight segment} of slope
$1/T_t$ joining $e_o$ to $e_d$ (Section~\ref{sec:firstorder}). At $T=T_t$ the
exponent $s(e)-e/T_t$ is therefore \emph{constant} across the whole segment: to
leading order in $V$ the maximum is degenerate along an interval, not at two
points. The degeneracy is lifted at the next order. Any configuration with $e$
strictly inside the interval is a phase-separated one and must contain an
interface, whose free-energy cost is $2\sigma L^{d-1}$ --- subextensive, hence
invisible in $s(e)$, but a genuine \emph{penalty} on the interior:
\[
\ln P(e)=\frac{V}{k_B}\Big(s(e)-\frac{e}{T_t}\Big)
-\frac{2\sigma L^{d-1}}{k_BT_t}\Big|_{\text{interior}}+\dots
\]
The two endpoints, being single-phase and interface-free, escape the penalty.
Hence \textbf{exactly two peaks}, at $e_o$ and $e_d$, and the dip between them has
depth $\ln(P_{\max}/P_{\min})\simeq2\sigma L^{d-1}/k_BT_t$ --- which is precisely
the Lee--Kosterlitz barrier quoted above, now derived rather than asserted.
\end{itemize}
The count is therefore the number of \emph{distinct energy densities} among the
coexisting phases. Note the consequence for symmetric models: at a first-order
transition of the $q$-state Potts model the $q$ ordered states are related by
symmetry and so share one energy density $e_o$, giving $P(E)$ \textbf{two} peaks,
not $q+1$.

\paragraph{$P(\phi)$: count the degenerate minima, which means count the broken
symmetry.} Here $\varphi$ is the constrained free-energy density $f(T;\phi)$ of
Section~\ref{sec:minimum}, defined by the restricted trace
$e^{-\beta Vf(\phi)}=\Tr\big[\delta(\hat\phi-\phi)e^{-\beta\hat H}\big]$, so that
literally $P(\phi)\propto e^{-\beta Vf(\phi)}$ and the histogram \emph{is} the
constrained free energy. The number of peaks is then a group-theoretic question,
not an empirical one. If the Hamiltonian is invariant under a symmetry group $G$
and the ordered state is invariant only under a subgroup $H\subset G$, the set of
degenerate minima is the coset space $G/H$ --- the order-parameter manifold ---
and
\[
\#\ \text{ordered peaks}=|G/H| .
\]
This immediately answers the question of what happens when the order parameter is
not binary:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Ising, $G=\mathbb{Z}_2$}, $H=\{e\}$: $|G/H|=2$, peaks at $\pm m_s$.
\emph{This} is where ``bimodal'' comes from. It is a fact about
$\mathbb{Z}_2$, not a general truth.
\item \textbf{$q$-state Potts or $\mathbb{Z}_q$ clock, $T<T_c$}: $q$ degenerate
ordered states, hence \textbf{$q$ peaks}. For $q=3$ these sit at the vertices of
a triangle in the two-component order-parameter plane.
\item \textbf{XY, $G=U(1)$}: $G/H$ is a \emph{circle}. There are no discrete
peaks at all --- $P(\mathbf{m})$ is supported on a degenerate ring
$|\mathbf{m}|=m_s$ with the phase free. Beware: the marginal distribution of a
single component $m_x$ is double-peaked, which is a projection artifact and not
two phases.
\item \textbf{Heisenberg, $G=O(3)$}: $G/H=S^{2}$, a spherical shell of maxima.
\item \textbf{$T>T_c$}: $f$ has a single minimum at $\phi=0$; one peak.
\item \textbf{$T=T_c$ exactly}: $f$ is flat to quartic order at the fixed point
and the concentration argument fails, since the correlation length is comparable
to $L$. $P(\phi)$ is then the \emph{universal critical distribution}, a broad,
weakly double-humped shape --- neither the two sharp peaks of $T<T_c$ nor the
single Gaussian of $T>T_c$. Its shape is a universal function, and its fourth
cumulant is what the Binder crossing below exploits.
\item \textbf{Symmetry-breaking first-order transition at $T_t$} (Potts with
$q>4$ in $d=2$, $q\geq3$ in $d=3$): the $q$ ordered minima and the disordered
minimum at $\phi=0$ are \emph{all} degenerate in free energy at $T_t$, since that
is what ``the transition temperature'' means. Hence \textbf{$q+1$ peaks}. Two
provisos: this holds only \emph{at} $T_t$ (just off it, one set of minima is
lower and the others are metastable), and only for a first-order transition that
also breaks a symmetry. A first-order transition with no symmetry breaking ---
liquid--gas --- has just \textbf{two} peaks in $P(\rho)$.
\item \textbf{The $q=2$ caveat.} There is no first-order $q=2$ case: the
two-state Potts model \emph{is} the Ising model, whose transition is continuous in
every dimension $d\geq2$. The formula $q+1$ therefore never yields three peaks
for Ising, and one should not read the table as claiming so.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The instances, collected.}
\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
Situation & $P(E)$ & $P(\phi)$ \\
\midrule
Ising, $T>T_c$ & unimodal & unimodal at $\phi=0$ \\
Ising, $T<T_c$ & unimodal & bimodal, $\pm m_s$ \\
Ising, $T=T_c$ & unimodal, anomalously broad & universal double-humped \\
XY / Heisenberg, $T<T_c$ & unimodal & ring $S^{1}$ / shell $S^{2}$ \\
$q$-state Potts, continuous, $T<T_c$ & unimodal & $q$-modal \\
$q$-state Potts, first order, $T=T_t$ & bimodal ($e_o,e_d$) & $(q{+}1)$-modal \\
Liquid--gas, first order, $T=T_t$ & bimodal ($e_\ell,e_g$) & bimodal ($\rho_\ell,\rho_g$) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Figure~\ref{fig:histograms} shows the characteristic shapes, and
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising} computes every entry of the Ising rows explicitly in
mean-field theory, where $P(m)$, $P(E)$, $c$, $\chi$ and $U_4$ are all available
in closed form, and Appendix~\ref{sec:apppotts} does the same for the $q$-state
Potts rows, including the $(q{+}1)$-modal $P(\phi)$ and the bimodal $P(E)$.

\paragraph{A finite-size proviso.} All of the multi-peak statements describe the
\emph{equilibrium} distribution. In a finite system the peaks are separated by
barriers $\sim2\sigma L^{d-1}$, so the time to tunnel between them grows as
$e^{2\sigma L^{d-1}/k_BT}$; a simulation that does not cross the barrier will
report one peak regardless. In the thermodynamic limit the barriers are infinite,
the ensemble is non-ergodic, and one must choose a sector by hand --- which is
just spontaneous symmetry breaking, and the same statement as the ensemble
inequivalence of Section~\ref{sec:firstorder}.

\paragraph{Why both facts are used in practice.} Two different cumulants exploit
them. The fourth-order magnetisation cumulant
$U_4=1-\langle\phi^4\rangle/3\langle\phi^2\rangle^2$ takes the value $0$ for a
single Gaussian ($T>T_c$) and $2/3$ for two sharp symmetric peaks ($T<T_c$), so
at $T_c$ it sits at a universal intermediate value and its curves for different
$L$ cross there --- the Binder crossing that locates $T_c$. At a first-order
transition it instead develops a minimum that diverges negatively with $L$,
because the $\phi=0$ peak coexists with the ordered ones. The \emph{energy}
cumulant $V_L=1-\langle E^4\rangle/3\langle E^2\rangle^2$ stays at $2/3$ through a
continuous transition and deviates at a first-order one --- precisely because
$P(E)$ is bimodal there and unimodal here.
